Inflation does not move the rate, although chapter 16 lists the normal rate of return among the things "affected by inflation". The module has one rate, where chapter 13 says that each type of instrument had its own. The holders of bonds do not appear as a group that presses on government, so the rule states the outcome and not the contest. The paper's link from sabotage to systemic fear is left to a later module. The debt module reckons the government's debt as a fixed interest payment divided by the normal rate of return, so a lower rate enlarges that debt and leaves the interest unchanged.

**Expected.** Before the change of conduct nothing should differ from a run without the module. After it, the rate should fall some years after every period of slow hiring and rise some years after every boom. All capitalization and the power index should then move in the opposite direction to the rate, by the proportion of the customary rate to the current rate. With the strength of part 4 set to zero, differential capitalization, unemployment and the capitalist share of income should be exactly what they are without the module, because the rate drops out of every ratio. With part 4 on, a high rate should slow hiring, and slow hiring should lower the rate five years later. A rule that answers its own effect after a delay can produce a swing of its own, so a cycle in the rate and in unemployment is possible. A long crisis should be followed by a lower rate and a higher power index, which is the direction of the paper's reading of the United States after the 1980s. The size of every one of these movements depends on the slope, which has no source, so only their direction and their timing can be read as results.

**What it showed.** These results were obtained on 26 November 2026 with the slope at its new default of 1. They come from runs at the full count of 300,000 starting claims over 119 years, compared with the same run without the module. Popular resistance passed 0.3 in the year labelled 1939, and every printed figure up to 1940 was identical in the two runs. After that the rate followed the bouts of stagflation and the booms between them. It peaked at 5.9, 5.9 and 6.0 per cent in 1953, 1974 and 1991, and it fell to 4.1, 4.0 and 4.2 per cent in 1964, 1981 and 2005. The lowest and the highest allowed rate were never reached. At the readings taken every ten years, the power index stood between 0.87 and 1.18 of its value at the customary rate. Differential capitalization was 8,122 in 1950 against 8,121 without the module, 15,563 against 14,374 in 1970, 19,941 against 16,481 in 1990 and 21,983 against 17,131 in 2005. These differences do not show a steady gain for dominant capital. They arise because the later bouts came about five years earlier with the module on: stagflation was at its height near 1975 and 2000, where the run without the module has it near 1980 and 2005. In 2005 unemployment was 0.5 per cent with the module and 6.0 per cent without it, for the same reason.

The same run was repeated with the old slope of 4, to show what depends on the slope. The turning points of the rate fell in the same years, within two years. The size of the swing grew in proportion: the rate peaked at 8.6, 9.0 and 9.2 per cent and fell to 1.1, 1.0 and 2.4 per cent, and it touched the lowest allowed rate in 1982. At the readings taken every ten years, the power index then stood between 0.65 and 2.07 of its value at the customary rate. The direction and the timing of the movements are therefore produced by the rule, and their size is whatever the slope makes it.

Smaller trials at 60,000 starting claims over 119 years used slopes of 0, 0.5, 1, 2 and 4. These are reduced counts, and their figures are for comparison only. With a slope of 0 every figure was identical to the run without the module. With the strength of part 4 set to zero and the slope at 1, differential capitalization, unemployment and the capitalist share of income equalled those of the run without the module in every year, to within the rounding error of the computer (one part in a thousand trillion). The rate moved between 4.2 and 5.5 per cent at a slope of 0.5, between 3.4 and 5.9 per cent at 1, between 2.3 and 6.8 per cent at 2, and between 1.0 and 8.7 per cent at 4. Only the slope of 4 reached the lowest allowed rate. Two runs at 20,000 claims over 80 years, with the modules listed in opposite orders, gave identical figures.

Five things are doubtful. The size of every movement rests on a setting with no source. A change of one point in the rate moved the later bouts of stagflation by about five years at the full count, so the timing of bouts is very sensitive to small pushes, and a comparison of two runs in a single year can mislead. The swing of thirteen to nineteen years that the old slope produced at 60,000 claims was much weaker at a slope of 1: after a first peak of 5.9 per cent in 1943 and a low of 4.3 per cent in 1952, the rate stayed between 4.5 and 5.1 per cent until the last crisis. The paper's reversal of 1939 did not appear: the power index moved against the lagged growth of employment both before the change of conduct and after it (correlations of -0.60 and -0.32 on ten-year averages at 60,000 claims), where the paper reports that the two moved together before 1939. The lag of five years, the average of ten years and the twenty years of part 2 are still figures from the paper's account of one country.

**What it showed with the earlier slope of 4.** This paragraph is kept as a record of the runs made before the slope was changed. These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 and 119 years, compared with the same runs without the module. Popular resistance passed 0.3 in the year labelled 1928, at the height of the first bout of stagflation, and every figure before that year was identical in the two runs. In a trial with the strength of part 4 set to zero, differential capitalization, unemployment and the capitalist share of income were identical to the run without the module in all 119 years, while the rate rose to 8.7 per cent in 1943 after the rush of hiring that followed the first bout and fell to the lowest level the rule allows, 1 per cent, in the crisis of the 2000s, so the power index stood at 0.58 of its value at the customary rate in 1943 and at five times that value at the end. With part 4 on, the rate and unemployment swung together without any bout of stagflation between 1950 and 1990: the rate peaked at 8.7, 6.9, 6.0 and 5.5 per cent in 1943, 1962, 1975 and 1993 and fell to 1.5, 2.1 and 4.1 per cent in 1952, 1966 and 1984, and unemployment moved between 0.5 and 7.5 per cent where the run without the module rested near 6.2 per cent. The high rate of the 1940s deepened the small bout of that decade, with unemployment of 8.8 per cent in 1946 against 6.7 per cent and differential capitalization of 787 in 1947 against 964, and the last bout began about four years later than in the run without the module and ended with the rate at 1 per cent in both trials. Four things are doubtful: the swing of thirteen to nineteen years is probably a product of the rule's own memory of fifteen years, in the same way as the cycle of the compass, and it faded with each turn; the first rise of the rate depends on the fall of unemployment to the model's floor after a bout, which the README lists as a fault; the rate reached its lowest allowed level, so that setting decided the size of the final rise in the power index; and the power index moved against the lagged growth of employment both before and after the change of conduct (correlations of -0.62 and -0.52 on ten-year averages), so the paper's reversal of 1939 did not appear. Two runs at 20,000 claims with the modules listed in opposite orders gave the same figures to within rounding error.

---

## Optional module: Protections as barred entry

The module is in `modules/protections.py` and is switched on with `--with protections`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. Two optional reads were added. The sabotage module now multiplies the extra hiring that a high markup allows small claims by a number that this module publishes for each industry. The green-field module multiplies the founding of new claims by the same number. The number is 1 when this module is off, and a run without this module gives exactly the figures it gave before. `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** Chapter 12, "The differential underpinnings of universal sabotage" and "Immaterial assets". Chapter 13, "Government capitalized" with its footnote on Microsoft. Chapter 9, the passage on law and religion. Chapter 14, "The figurative identity" and the passage on exclusion. Chapter 15, the footnote to "Differential advantage". Chapter 16, "Cost cutting". Bichler and Nitzan, *The Global Political Economy of Israel* (2002), chapters 3, 4 and 6. Bichler and Nitzan, "Dominant Capital and the New Wars" (2004). Joseph Baines and Sandy Brian Hager, "Rentiership and Intellectual Monopoly in Contemporary Capitalism" (2023), which was downloaded from the authors' archive for this module and is in `data/bn_archives/papers/baines_hager_rentiership_intellectual_monopoly_2023/`.

**What the literature says.** A protection here means a patent, a copyright, a licence, a concession or an exclusive contract. The book says that such a right is the thing that owners capitalize: "on its books, Microsoft – like any other corporation – capitalizes not the invention itself, but the patent or copyright that defends it". The reason is exclusion: "unless the innovation is protected, everyone can use it, and what everyone can use nobody can profit from". The conclusion is that "a corporation – no matter how 'innovative' it claims to be – can only capitalize the protection of knowledge, never the knowledge itself". The book places protections among the general means of beating the average. "For the profits of one owner (or a coalition of owners) to beat the average, others must be prevented from accessing the same earnings", and the means include "exclusive contracts", "patent and copyright laws" and "legal monopolies". The cost falls on others: "For the benefiting owner, the differential gain accrues because the necessary industrial limitation is borne by other owners." Where exclusion fails, earnings fail: "when exclusion cannot be ensured, like in the case of software development or the manufacturing of microchips, soaring production often overshoots into excess capacity and falling profits". Chapter 16 says that advantages without protection do not last. Final commodities "can often be protected through patents, copyrights and other exclusionary threats", while improvements in production "proliferate easily, and this rapid spread quickly dilutes the initial advantage". A protection is a tie to government. The owners of Microsoft "can profit differentially from this software only insofar as they can prevent others from using it without pay", and this depends "on the extent to which Microsoft can harness the government apparatus to its own end". The book then asks what would follow if the protection were withdrawn: "The most likely result is that Microsoft's earnings and capitalization will drop sharply if not collapse altogether. The magnitude of the drop represents the extent to which Microsoft capitalizes the government." Enforcement matters as much as the law. Businesses that deal in patents and copyrights "capitalize both the law and the extent to which it is enforced", and where copying is not punished the governments' "contribution to Microsoft's bottom line is likely to remain negligible". The 2002 book gives cases of grants and renewals. In Israel in the 1950s the government worked "through the selective handout of exclusive concessions, certificates and licences", and "these property rights quickly turned into gold mines for those lucky enough to get them". In the 1990s the cable companies fought "to renew their exclusive free licence to deliver cable services for another 15 years" and "to extend this licence into internet and telephone services", and each side had its own minister. The same book says that "if technology advances in leaps and bounds, but its protection consolidates even faster, the result would be fatter margins". Baines and Hager write that "without the legal protection that patents provide to make their products artificially scarce, the rents of pharmaceutical companies would quickly evaporate". The book also warns against reading too much into one grant: "The fact that a certain corporation was granted a patent, that it had the government move to its side … cannot, in and of itself, tell us much about that company's rate of differential accumulation." Size helps the tie, because consolidation "makes it easier for firms to collude as well as to fuse with government organs". Small owners are not shut out in principle: "there is nothing to prevent … a university dropout from incorporating Microsoft". The literature gives no rule and no rate for who obtains a protection, how much it raises a markup, or how often it is renewed.

**Rule.** The rule has seven parts.

1. Each claim has a tie to government organs, between 0 and 1. The tie is the claim's capitalization divided by the sum of that capitalization and one hundred times the capitalization of the average claim. A claim of average size has a tie near 0.01. A claim one hundred times the average has a tie of one half.
2. Each year a claim without a protection obtains one with a probability of 0.2 per cent plus 10 per cent times its tie. A protection is granted for fifteen years.
3. Each year the module sets the markup of every protected claim to one and a half times the markup of the unprotected claims of the same industry. The price of the claim moves with its markup, and the wage does not. In the year of the grant the claim gives up employees in proportion to the rise of its price.
4. The module publishes, for each industry, the part of the industry that is not under protection. The part under protection is the protected claims' part of the industry's employees. In that proportion the green-field module founds fewer new claims, and the sabotage module allows small claims less of the extra hiring that a markup above the normal level brings.
5. When the fifteen years are over, the protection is renewed for another fifteen years with a probability equal to the claim's tie. Otherwise it lapses, and the markup of the claim falls in the same year to the markup of the unprotected claims of its industry.
6. Popular resistance weakens enforcement. The excess markup of part 3 and the bar of part 4 are both multiplied by one minus the level of popular resistance.
7. The module publishes the part of all employees that works for protected claims, the part of the hundred largest claims that hold a protection, the average tie of the hundred largest, the markup of protected claims against their unprotected neighbours at the start of the year, and the numbers of grants, renewals and lapses.

The module changes earnings per employee, price and, in the year of a grant, employees. It does not change hype or risk. The years that a protection has left are kept as an added number on each claim. In a merger the buyer keeps its own number. A protected buyer therefore extends its protection over what it buys, and the protection of an acquired claim ends.

**Additions.** The form of every part is an addition, and every number is an addition. The term of fifteen years is taken from one case: it is the renewal that the Israeli cable companies sought for their licence in the 1990s. The literature gives no general term, so the setting is an addition. The other numbers are the excess markup of one half, the two grant rates, the renewal probability equal to the tie, the size of one hundred average claims at which the tie is one half, the complete bar on entry in an industry wholly under protection, and the loss of half a per cent of employees for each per cent of price rise in the year of the grant, which is copied from the sabotage module. Measuring a claim's tie by its size against the average claim is an addition. Letting popular resistance weaken enforcement is an addition, and it copies the form that the sabotage module uses for the hold of dominant capital. Measuring the bar on entry by the protected claims' part of the industry's employees is an addition. Five things in the literature are missing. A protection does not lower the risk of its holder, although chapter 13 says that government power bears on "earnings and risk". Rival claims do not contest a grant or a renewal, although the cable case is such a contest. A dominant claim cannot buy a small claim in order to take over its protection, although the 2002 book describes large firms buying new technology "through corporate takeover", because the core lets a buyer keep only its own added numbers. All protections have the same strength and the same term. Protections do not differ between countries, because the model has one country.

**Expected.** Early in a run all claims are of similar size, every tie is near zero, and a few small claims should obtain protections and lose them fifteen years later. As mergers make some claims very large, their ties should approach 1, and they should obtain protections and keep them. Dominant capital's earnings per employee should then rise against the average, and differential capitalization should be higher than without the module. The capitalist share of income should rise, because protected prices rise and the wage does not. The cost cutting module should wear the excess markup down each year, and this module should restore it, so the cost cutting module should be credited with a loss. In an industry with protected claims the average markup stands above the normal level. Small claims should then hire more, and that hiring should push the markup of the unprotected claims below the normal level, so the gain of the protected claims should fall partly on their neighbours. The bar on entry should limit that hiring and should raise unemployment a little. In a bout of stagflation popular resistance should weaken enforcement and narrow the excess markup.

**What it showed.** These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 years, compared with the same run without the module. Protections sorted themselves by size: in each five years between 900 and 3,800 protections were granted and, once the first terms had run out, between 800 and 2,500 lapsed, while only 20 to 60 were renewed, and by the year labelled 1920 the average tie of the hundred largest claims was 0.82 and 72 per cent of them held a protection, rising to 94 per cent by 1950. Dominant capital's earnings per employee stood at 2.06 times the average in 1920 against 1.59 without the module and at 2.68 against 1.75 in 1950, differential capitalization was 550 against 445 and 2,261 against 1,564, and the capitalist share of income was 12.8 against 10.9 per cent and 18.8 against 12.2 per cent. The markup of protected claims stood at about 1.37 times that of their neighbours and not at the 1.5 the rule sets, because the cost cutting module wears the difference down every year, and it narrowed to about 1.2 while popular resistance was near 0.45. A trial with the excess markup set to 1 raised the capitalist share to 40 per cent by 1950, and a trial of that setting without the bar on entry gave almost the same share but unemployment of 0.6 per cent in 1950 against 3.7 per cent, so the bar acted on hiring and hardly on the share. Four things are doubtful: nearly all of the hundred largest claims end up protected, so the module becomes a standing addition to the markup of dominant capital; the capitalist share did not fall back after the bout of stagflation as far as it does without the module; a newly founded claim copies the average markup of its industry, which includes part of the excess; and the modules for price leaders and for the public debt read or reset the same markups and were not tried together with this one. Two runs at 20,000 claims over 40 years with the modules listed in opposite orders gave identical figures.

**Changed on 26 November 2026.** The module now owns a markup component named "protection premium" (see "How modules share a quantity"). The premium of a protected claim is 1.5 under full enforcement, and the premium of every other claim is 1. Before the change the module set the whole markup of a protected claim to 1.5 times the average markup of its unprotected neighbours. It now multiplies the claim's own markup, so a protected claim keeps its own cost-cutting edge and its own lead from price rises. A lapse returns the premium to 1. The cost cutting module no longer wears the premium down, so the statements in this section that it does, and that it is credited with a loss, are no longer true. Before the change the two modules worked against each other every year: cost cutting drew the unprotected neighbours up toward a level that the protected claims had raised, and this module then set the protected claims at 1.5 times the new level of the neighbours. That ratchet is gone, and the effect of the module is smaller. The premium is a member of the overlap group "hold on buyers", and the module now runs together with the price leaders module. The figures under "What it showed" come from before the change. In a run at 60,000 starting claims over 60 years, the capitalist share in the year labelled 1920 was 11.4 per cent after the change and 12.8 before it, and differential earnings per employee were 1.78 against 2.06. In the year labelled 1950 differential capitalization was 1,165 against 2,261 and the capitalist share was 15.5 per cent against 18.8. The run after the change had a second bout of stagflation in the 1940s, which the run before it did not have, and part of the difference in 1950 comes from that.

---

## Optional module: Privatisation

The module is in `modules/privatisation.py` and is switched on with `--with privatisation`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. It needs module 7, because it reads cohesion, and module 8, because it reads popular resistance. Two optional reads were added to `modules/greenfield.py`. The green-field module now adds to the working population a number that this module publishes, and it sets aside the privatised jobs before it lets other claims hire. Both numbers are 0 when this module is off, and a run without this module gives exactly the figures it gave before. `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** Chapter 17, "The role of the Middle East", the passage on the breadth phase that began in the late 1980s, with its footnote on government expenditure. Chapter 9, the passage on the two trajectories of the price system. Chapter 4, the passage on the welfare state since the 1970s. Chapter 5, "Public management". Chapter 15, "Green-field". Bichler and Nitzan, *The Global Political Economy of Israel* (2002), chapters 1, 3 and 6. Bichler and Nitzan, "Regimes of Differential Accumulation" (2001), the conclusion. Nitzan and Bichler, "New Imperialism or New Capitalism?" (2006). The search covered the book and the papers on disk. The online archive was not searched for this module.

**What the literature says.** The book treats privatisation as a way for dominant capital to grow in size. Of the late 1980s it says: "the ideological demise of public ownership and the 'mixed economy' opened the door for privatization of state assets and government services, which, from the viewpoint of dominant capital, was tantamount to green-field investment". It adds that "the combination of expansion into less developed countries, privatization and corporate amalgamation helped sustain a powerful breadth drive for large Western corporations despite the lacklustre growth of their 'parent' countries". The footnote gives the size of what was there to take and says who took it. Government expenditures "have risen from 14 per cent of GDP in the 1960s to 17 per cent in the 1980s, and remained more or less at that level since then", and "the privatization of such services — including transportation, water, infrastructure, education and security — typically takes the form of giving/selling them to dominant capital, which in turn contributes to differential accumulation in a manner similar to green-field investment". The 2002 book says the same in other words. "Part of this involved the sale of government-owned enterprises, but there was a much bigger prize here, namely the privatisation of government itself." "Much of this activity could be turned over to private hands; and since these usually ended up being the largest firms, the effect was tantamount to boosting differential accumulation through external breadth." The 2002 book also describes the terms. State assets went to favoured owners "at bargain prices". The group of state-owned firms in Israel "functioned more as a storage facility for privatisation targets, than as an accumulation-driven organisation". In privatisation, the thing that let money make money "was often having the right political friends". Chapter 9 says that the price system spread along two paths, one of which "privatized and priced more and more social activities". On what holds the door shut, the literature speaks of ideology and opposition. Chapter 4 says that with opposition neutralised "there has been little to prevent the wholesale spread of capitalism", and that "large chunks of the public domain have been privatized and handed over to the capitalists". Chapter 5 says that "once communism collapsed in the late 1980s, government officials and politicians around the world seemed all too eager to dismantle their own welfare states". The same chapter says that the public sector, "no matter how large and active, remained subservient to the logic of laissez faire individualism and the interests of its large capitalists". The 2001 paper counts "the privatization of state-owned firms and public services" as part of "the global pool of takeover targets". The literature gives no rule and no rate for how fast public services are taken over. It does not say what built the public services in the first place.

**Rule.** The rule has seven parts.

1. The module keeps a pool of publicly run employment. The people in the pool work for government organs and for no claim. They are outside the ledger, and they are not part of the working population that the ledger counts. At the start of a run the pool is 5 per cent of that working population. Each year the pool grows at the rate at which the working population grows.
2. Each year the module works out how far the door to the pool is open. The door is the cohesion of dominant capital, times one minus the defence. The defence is twice the level of popular resistance, and it is never more than 1. The door is shut when cohesion is zero, and it is shut when popular resistance is one half or more.
3. Dominant capital plans to take over, in the following year, 30 per cent of the pool times the door. The plan cannot exceed a quarter of the employees that the hundred largest claims already have. The module publishes the plan.
4. In the following year the planned number of employees leaves the pool and goes onto the books of the hundred largest claims. Each of those claims receives a part in proportion to the employees it already has. Each keeps its earnings per employee and its price, so the services carry a markup once a claim holds them. The green-field module adds the same number of people to the working population of the ledger, and it sets the same number aside before it lets other claims hire. Since 26 November 2026 dominant capital pays a price. The worth of a takeover is the capitalization it adds to the hundred largest claims. The price is one half of the worth. The module publishes the price as the year's proceeds, and module 2 retires government debt with it in the following year.
5. The takeover is not counted as growth of capacity in any industry. The part of the sabotage rule that wears markups down where capacity grows does not see it.
6. While popular resistance is above zero, government organs plan to hire some of the people who are out of work. The number is 10 per cent of those people, times the level of popular resistance. In the following year those people leave the working population of the ledger and join the pool.
7. The module publishes the pool as a part of all working people, the door, the planned takeover and the planned public hiring as parts of the working population, and the part of the top hundred's employees that came from the pool.

The module changes the ledger only by scaling the employees of the hundred largest claims. The price of the takeover changes no claim: it lowers the government's debt that module 2 reports.

**Additions.** The literature supplies three things: the receivers are dominant capital, the gain works like green-field growth, and the door opened when opposition to it collapsed. Every number in the rule is an addition. The starting pool of 5 per cent is an addition. The book's figure of 14 to 17 per cent of gross domestic product is for the world from the 1960s, and it is a record to compare with and not a setting. The form of the door is an addition, and so is the figure of twice the resistance. The yearly 30 per cent and the limit of a quarter are additions. Sharing the takeover among the hundred largest claims in proportion to their employees is an addition. Part 5 is an interpretation. The book says that green-field growth threatens prices because it adds capacity, and privatisation hires without building, but the book does not draw that conclusion itself. Part 6 is wholly an addition. It was added so that the pool has a source inside the model, and its rate can be set to zero. Letting the pool grow with the working population is an addition. Six things were missing when the module was built. The first two were that nothing was paid for the takeover, although the literature says "giving/selling", and that the government's debt was not reduced by any proceeds. Both were dealt with on 26 November 2026, as the last paragraph of this section describes. The taxes that pay public employees are not recorded, so the module cannot show the payments that government organs go on making to the claims that run the services. The sale of a state-owned firm as a whole claim is not modelled. The pool has no industries, and privatised employees take the industry of the claim that receives them. The book places privatisation in a breadth phase, and the rule does not shut the door during stagflation except through the resistance that stagflation provokes.

**Expected.** Early in a run the top hundred hold little and cohesion is near zero, so the takeover should be slow. As mergers raise concentration, cohesion should rise and the door should open further, so privatisation should add to a merger wave and not replace it. The top hundred's part of all employees and differential capitalization should both be higher than without the module. The gain should be credited to privatisation and should resemble the gain from green-field growth, without the fall in the markup that hiring brings. Higher concentration should bring the first bout of stagflation sooner or make it stronger, because the compass allows as much stagflation as cohesion permits. During a bout, popular resistance should shut the door and public hiring should refill the pool. Public hiring should lower unemployment during the bout, and lower unemployment should let wages gain on prices. After the bout the door should stay nearly shut while resistance fades, and it should open again when unemployment next rises and cohesion with it.

**What it showed.** These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 years, compared with the same run without the module. The pool fell from 5.0 per cent of all working people to 2.6 per cent by the year labelled 1920 as the door opened from 0.02 to 0.17, and by then one eighth of the top hundred's employees had come from the pool, their part of all employees was 21.1 per cent against 18.4 per cent, and differential capitalization was 501 against 445. The gain credited to privatisation was about 0.05 in each five years at first and 0.02 by 1920, beside 0.63 falling to 0.14 for mergers. The first bout of stagflation was stronger, with an intensity of 1.00 against 0.90 in 1925 and a capitalist share of income of 24.1 against 20.2 per cent, and it ended sooner, with unemployment of 1.1 per cent in 1930 against 5.2 per cent. During and after the bout popular resistance near one half shut the door, and public hiring raised the pool again to 3.1 per cent by 1935; a trial with public hiring switched off left the pool at 1.4 per cent and gave nearly the same figures before the bout. Three things are doubtful. The door opens widest in the first year or two of a bout, when unemployment has raised cohesion and resistance has not yet risen, which is the opposite of the book's placing of privatisation in a breadth phase. After 1930 the two runs differ in the timing of later bouts, and that difference cannot be traced to privatisation alone. The organised force module limits its own hiring without knowing of public hiring, and the two were not tried together. Two runs at 20,000 claims over 40 years with the modules listed in opposite orders gave the same figures to within rounding error.

**Changed on 26 November 2026.** The takeover now has a price. The book says that services are handed over by "giving/selling them to dominant capital" (chapter 17, the footnote on government expenditure). The 2002 book says that state assets went "at bargain prices", that one buyer "ended up getting it for a song", and that the state planned to "plow some of the privatisation proceeds" into a fund (*The Global Political Economy of Israel*, chapters 1 and 6). The proceeds therefore go to the government, and the price is below the worth. The literature gives no figure. The module has a new setting, `price_part`, the part of the worth that is paid. It is a plain setting with no source. Its default of one half is the middle between giving the services away, which is 0, and selling them at their full worth, which is 1. Using the proceeds to retire government debt is an addition. The payment changes no claim's earnings, because the model counts a claim's debt and equity together and does not follow its owners. Its effect is on the government's debt, and through the public debt module on the tax for interest. The earnings on the privatised employees are a markup, which whoever buys the services pays in the price. The run with this module alone gives the same figures as before in every year at 300,000 claims. Over 60 years the proceeds retired debt worth 2 per cent of a year's income. The full account is under "The record of transfers" in the section "How modules share a quantity".

---

## Optional module: Specific barriers won by resistance

The module is in `modules/barriers.py` and is switched on with `--with barriers`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. It can be run together with the boundary contest module. Two optional reads were added. The merger module now multiplies the openness of the sector boundary and of the national boundary, as each buyer sees them, by one minus a number that this module publishes for the buyer's sector. The boundary contest module now multiplies its hold by one minus a second number that this module publishes. Both numbers are 0 when this module is off, and a run without this module gives exactly the figures it gave before. `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** Chapter 15, "Three transformations" with its footnote on the Glass-Steagall Act, and "Breaking the envelope". Chapter 13, on corporations and government organs as bodies of one mode of power. Bichler and Nitzan, "Regimes of Differential Accumulation" (2001), the conclusion. Bichler and Nitzan, *The Global Political Economy of Israel* (2002), chapter 2, footnote 24, and chapter 6, "The Brodet Report" and "Releasing Value". Nitzan and Bichler, "Going Global: Differential Accumulation and the Great U-turn in South Africa and Israel" (2001), which was downloaded from the authors' archive for this module and is in `data/bn_archives/papers/going_global_2001/`.

**What the literature says.** The book says that mergers produce their own obstacles: amalgamation "is restricted by the ability of broader state institutions to quickly accommodate the new corporate formations", and "as amalgamation builds up momentum, it also generates higher and higher roadblocks, contradictions and counter-forces". The footnote to that sentence gives the examples: "The 1933 Glass-Steagall Act, for instance, barred US banks from making industrial investments, a prohibition that only recently has been relaxed. Similar transformational effects were brought on by the post-war dismantling of the Japanese Zaibatsu, by the 1990s unbundling of South African holding groups and the divestment of Israeli banks of their 'non-financial' holdings". The examples are of two kinds. The first kind bars a group from buying outside its line of business. The second kind makes a group sell what it already holds outside that line. The 2001 paper on regimes expects more barriers of the first kind: "In many sectors global amalgamation is approaching 'antitrust thresholds' as they are now called, and in some of them … it has already triggered regulatory intervention." The paper on South Africa and Israel says that mergers are limited "by societal and technological barriers on corporate concentration". It describes the South African unbundling as one side of a pact with the African National Congress and the unions: "dominant capital accepts the need to unbundle its oligopolistic 'stranglehold' over the economy (selling parts of it to black entrepreneurs, including the unions) in return for gradual capital decontrols and the ability to diversify abroad". The 2002 book describes the Israeli case. A government committee required the banks to "reduce their holdings in any 'real' company to 20 per cent or less". The authors comment that "the great octopus of domestic assets had to be first chopped down into vendible units", that the committee "said nothing on how to prevent the divested companies from being re-amalgamated into different groupings", and that "the real purpose of the report was never to lower concentration, but to change its nature". On the wearing down of barriers the literature offers the words "only recently has been relaxed", the remark that the Israeli rules "could be easily circumvented", and two statements of chapter 15: "amalgamation is simply too important to give up", and the refocusing of firms on their core activities "is bound to become exhausted, pushing dominant capital back toward conglomeration". The literature gives no rule for which group a barrier falls on, how fast a barrier rises, or how it is worn down. It gives no rates.

**Rule.** A barrier is a number between 0 and 1 for each sector. It applies to the dominant claims whose own industry lies in that sector. The line of business of a claim is its industry. The rule has seven parts.

1. The module keeps an estimate of what each dominant claim has bought outside its own industry. It numbers every claim, so that it can see which claims disappeared during the last year. Every claim that disappeared was bought, because nothing else in the model removes a claim. The module does not know which buyer took which claim. It divides the employees of the claims that disappeared from each industry among the dominant claims of that year, in proportion to the chance that each was the buyer under the rule of the merger module. The employees bought in other industries are the outside holdings of a claim.
2. The counter-force is the level of roadblocks plus half the level of popular resistance, and it is never more than 1. The exposure of a sector is the outside holdings of its dominant claims as a part of all employees in the economy, divided by 1 per cent, and it is never more than 1. Each year the barrier of a sector rises by 0.3 times the counter-force times the exposure.
3. The merger module multiplies the openness of the sector boundary and of the national boundary, as a buyer seated in the sector sees them, by one minus the barrier. Buying inside the buyer's own industry is not affected.
4. While the barrier of a sector stands above 0.5, each dominant claim seated in that sector sells a quarter of each of its outside holdings every year. A holding is sold whole when a quarter of it would be smaller than the average small claim. Each sold holding becomes a new claim in the industry where it was bought. The new claim takes the earnings per employee, price, hype and risk of the seller. The sale therefore changes who holds the employees and leaves total capitalization unchanged. Any dominant claim that is not held back by a barrier can buy the new claim again.
5. Each year a barrier falls by 5 per cent of its own height times the push of dominant capital on its boundaries. The push is the number that the boundary contest module publishes. Without that module the push is the part of its reach that dominant capital already holds. A barrier does not fade by itself.
6. The module keeps a running level of forced sales, measured as a part of the employees of dominant capital, and publishes it as the bargain. The boundary contest module lowers its hold on the next boundary in that proportion.
7. The module publishes the barrier of each sector, the average, highest and lowest barrier, the number of sectors under forced sale, the part of dominant capital's employees in outside holdings, the year's forced sales, and the bargain.

**Additions.** The form of every part is an addition, and every number is an addition. The numbers are the yearly rise of 0.3, the exposure that is complete at 1 per cent of all employees, the half weight of popular resistance, the yearly wear of 5 per cent, the threshold of 0.5 for forced sales, the quarter sold each year, and the two settings of the bargain. Four choices need to be stated. The book says that the population is "impartial to breadth", and the rule still lets popular resistance add to the counter-force, because the South African unbundling was won by a popular movement and the unions. That setting can be put to zero. The footnote speaks of banks and of holding groups, and the rule applies one barrier to all the dominant claims seated in a sector. Measuring exposure by the part of the whole economy that a sector's dominant claims hold outside their lines is an addition, and it rests loosely on the words "antitrust thresholds" and "stranglehold over the economy". The estimate in part 1 is a reckoning of chances and not a record. Five things in the literature are missing. A barrier does not stop outsiders from buying into a sector. The Zaibatsu were dismantled by an occupying power, and the rule has no place for one. The Israeli unbundling made room for foreign buyers, and the model has one country. The sold claims are not handed to particular new owners. A claim that was once dominant and is later bought loses its record of outside holdings.

**Expected.** While the sector boundary is shut, no dominant claim holds anything outside its industry, and no barrier should appear. A barrier that was never raised is then different from a boundary that never opened. Once the sector boundary begins to open, outside holdings should grow, and barriers should rise in the sectors whose dominant claims hold the most. A barrier should first slow the buying of those claims and should then force sales. Forced sales should lower the top hundred's part of all employees and should be credited with a loss of differential capitalization. Fewer mergers should lead the compass toward stagflation, because the book treats stagflation as what dominant capital turns to when mergers recede. Buyers held inside their lines should find fewer targets, so the pressure on the boundaries should rise, and that pressure should wear the barriers down. A barrier should therefore rise, stand for some decades, and fall. With the boundary contest module, forced sales should speed the opening of the next boundary.

**What it showed.** These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 and 119 years, compared with the same runs without the module. Until the year labelled 1920 the sector boundary was shut, no barrier appeared, and the figures were identical to those of the run without the module. By 1950 the barriers stood between 0.18 and 0.62 in different sectors, thirteen of the twenty sectors were under forced sale, sales in that year were 1.4 per cent of all employees, the top hundred held 21.8 per cent of employees against 24.4 per cent, and differential capitalization was 1,398 against 1,564, with a loss of 0.15 over five years credited to barriers. In the run of 119 years the barriers did not rise and then fall: from about 1965 they stayed between 0.43 and 0.58, close to the threshold for forced sales, sectors moved in and out of forced sale, and the top hundred held 8 per cent of employees in 2005 against 46 per cent, with bouts of stagflation near 1962, 1985 and 1998 where the run without the module had a single bout after 1950, near 2000. With the boundary contest module, over 60 years, the average barrier passed 0.5 by 1925 and stood near 0.95 from 1940, outside holdings fell from 48 to 5 per cent of dominant capital's employees, the top hundred held 14 per cent of employees in 1950 against 46 per cent, and the sector boundary stood at 0.77 in 1950 and never closed, where without this module it peaked near 0.39 and had closed by 1950. Four things are doubtful. Barriers arose in every sector that had a dominant claim, so they were less specific than the title promises. The wearing down was too weak at these settings to bring any barrier down for long, so the runs show a standing contest at the threshold and do not show the relaxation that the footnote reports. The level at which the barriers settle is the threshold for forced sales, which is a setting. The module was not tried together with the optional modules other than the boundary contest. Two runs at 20,000 claims over 50 years with the boundary contest module and with the modules listed in opposite orders gave identical figures.

**Changed on 26 November 2026.** A holding that a dominant claim is forced to sell becomes a new claim with every component of the seller's earnings per employee (see "How modules share a quantity"). Before the change it took the seller's earnings per employee as one number. A run of 25 years gave the same figures as before the change.

---

## Optional module: Proletarianization

The module is in `modules/proletarianization.py` and is switched on with `--with proletarianization`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. It changes the yearly growth rate of the working population, which is a shared number of the envelope, and every module that reads that number then reads the rate this module has set. One optional read was added to `modules/compass.py`. The compass now multiplies the cohesion of dominant capital by a number that this module publishes. The number is 1 when this module is off, and a run without this module gives exactly the figures it gave before. `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** Chapter 14, Table 14.2, "External Breadth: Green-field Investment". Chapter 17, the passage on the 1950s and 1960s. Chapter 12, the passage on Figure 12.3. Nitzan and Bichler, "Going Global: Differential Accumulation and the Great U-turn in South Africa and Israel" (2001). Bichler and Nitzan, *The Global Political Economy of Israel* (2002), chapters 1, 2 and 3. Bichler and Nitzan, "Regimes of Differential Accumulation" (2001), the conclusion. Bichler and Nitzan, "Dominant Capital and the New Wars" (2004). The search covered the book and every paper on disk. A search of the authors' online archive for the word found only papers that are already on disk.

**What the literature says.** The book names the ceiling in one sentence. Green-field growth "involves a net addition of employees", and "its upper ceiling is the extent of proletarianization". Proletarianization means the turning of people who live outside wage employment into people who must work for a wage. The 2001 paper on South Africa and Israel names two sources of this kind of growth: it "involves the redistribution of new demand, principally generated by population growth and by proletarianization", and it is "far more sustainable, particularly as long as significant portions of the population are yet to be proletarianized". The same paper says that a large outside population is not enough: "having a large rural population does not guarantee proletarianization, which can stall for cultural or religious reasons". In South Africa "roughly half the population is still rural and yet to be proletarianized", and the potential "is capped by lingering racial conflict, massive crime, growing corruption, and an AIDS epidemic". The paper also says what proletarianization does to dominant capital. "Periods of rapid population growth and proletarianization tend to loosen the internal cohesiveness of corporate coalitions, making differential price increases both more difficult and less desirable." "The situation changes markedly when population growth subsides or when proletarianization runs into barriers", and then "the core groups gravitate, with a mixture of fear and greed, toward depth". The 2002 book gives a case. After 1967 there was "a growing need 'for a cheap, mobile labour force, with no social rights'", and "this was achieved by the proletarianisation of the Palestinian population". The annexation "was tantamount to a massive injection of breadth", and "by the mid-1970s, with the integration complete, growth again plummeted". The same book speaks of "'proletarianisation-ready' populations in and outside one's own society", and of "the controlled proletarianisation of a rapidly growing immigrant population". Chapter 17 of the book says that people can be drawn in a second time: "the postwar rebuilding of Europe and Japan … in some sense was equivalent to the re-proletarianization of their societies", and "the result was a powerful breadth engine". The 2004 paper says that accumulation "during the nineteenth century was anchored largely in proletarianization", so the process was strongest when dominant capital was least concentrated. The 2001 paper on regimes says that "half of the world's population still lives pretty much outside the capitalist fold". Chapter 12 reports that United States population growth was 2.2 per cent a year between the Civil War and 1900 and 1.6 per cent between 1900 and the Great Depression. The literature gives no rule for how fast people are drawn in, and no rates.

**Rule.** The rule has nine parts.

1. The module keeps a count of people who live outside wage employment. They work for no claim, they are outside the ledger, and they are not part of the working population that the ledger counts. At the start of a run there is one such person for every person in the working population.
2. Both populations grow each year by 1 per cent through natural increase.
3. Each year the module works out the drive to draw people in. The drive is one minus unemployment divided by 8 per cent, and it is never below 0. The level of 8 per cent is called the sufficient reserve. At that level of unemployment employers have enough idle hands and draw nobody in. The drive is 1 when nobody is idle.
4. Popular resistance stalls the drawing. The barrier is twice the level of popular resistance, and it is never more than 1.
5. The number of people planned to be drawn in during the following year is 2 per cent of the outside population, times the drive, times one minus the barrier.
6. When unemployment is above the sufficient reserve, a fifth of the people who are idle beyond that reserve are planned to leave the working population in the following year. They join the outside population and can be drawn in again.
7. The module sets the growth rate of the working population for the following year. The rate is the natural increase, plus the planned drawing, minus the planned falling back, both measured as parts of the working population. In the first year of a run the rate given as a setting is still in force, and the module counts the people it implies as drawn from the outside population.
8. The module publishes a number that the compass multiplies into the cohesion of dominant capital. The number is 1 divided by the sum of 1 and 40 times the planned drawing, where the drawing is measured as a part of the working population. The number is 1 when nobody is drawn in.
9. The module publishes the outside population as a part of all people, the drive, the barrier, the planned drawing, the planned falling back, and the growth rate it has set.

The module adds no claim and changes no claim. It acts on the ledger only through the growth rate of the working population and through the cohesion that the compass publishes.

**Additions.** The literature supplies five things: the ceiling is the number of people not yet drawn in, the drawing answers a need for hands, the drawing can stall, the drawing loosens the cohesion of dominant capital while it lasts, and its end turns dominant capital toward raising prices. Every number in the rule is an addition. The numbers are the starting outside population, the natural increase of 1 per cent, the yearly 2 per cent, the sufficient reserve of 8 per cent, the weight of 2 on popular resistance, the fifth that falls back, and the weight of 40 on cohesion. The starting figure borrows the word "half" from two sentences that describe the world and South Africa near the year 2000. Those sentences say nothing about the country or the starting year of a run, so the starting figure is an addition. The form of the drive is an addition. It rests on the words "a growing need" and on nothing more exact. Measuring the barrier by popular resistance is an addition, because the literature names cultural and religious reasons and racial conflict, and the model holds one level of popular resistance. Part 6 is an addition in its form. The book's case of re-proletarianization followed a war, and the rule lets heavy unemployment do what the war did. Six things are missing. The natural increase is still a setting, so one part of the growth of the working population still comes from outside the model. The literature calls the drawn-in labour "cheap", and the model has one wage, so the drawing does not weaken wages. The literature describes confiscation of land, occupation and controlled immigration as the means of drawing people in, and the rule has no such means. The outside population does not resist on its own account. The outside population buys nothing, so the "new demand" of the 2001 paper appears only as new hands. The model has one country, so people drawn from abroad and people drawn from the land are not told apart.

**Expected.** At the start of a run unemployment is 5 per cent, the drive is a little under one half, and the working population should grow by about 1.75 per cent a year, close to the setting of 1.7 per cent that the module replaces. The growth rate should then fall year by year as the outside population shrinks beside the working population. Slower growth of the working population should mean less hiring and fewer new claims, so the top hundred should come to hold a larger part of all employees, and the average claim should shrink more slowly. During a bout of stagflation the drawing should stop, because unemployment is high and popular resistance is high, and some people should fall back. After a bout the drawing should resume only as popular resistance fades. The cohesion of dominant capital should be lower early in a run, when many people are being drawn in, and the difference should fade as the drawing slows.

**What it showed.** These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 and 119 years, compared with the same runs without the module. The growth rate that the module set fell from 1.72 per cent in the year labelled 1895 to 1.51 per cent in 1920, fell to 0.80 per cent during the first bout of stagflation, when the drive was zero and 0.2 per cent of the working population fell back each year, recovered to 1.4 per cent by 1945, and declined to 1.09 per cent by 2005. By 1950 employment was 19.1 million against 23.4 million, there were 268,000 claims against 395,000, the top hundred held 25.6 per cent of employees against 24.4 per cent, and differential capitalization was 1,112 against 1,564, because fewer small claims were founded to lower the average. In the run of 119 years the number of claims peaked near 305,000 around 1975 and then fell, the top hundred held 70 per cent of employees in 2005 against 46 per cent, and differential capitalization was 2,017 against 4,851. The ceiling itself was never reached: the outside population was still 31 per cent of all people in 2005, and the drawing slowed because unemployment stood near 6 per cent, which left a drive of 0.2, and because standing popular resistance of 0.15 to 0.25 kept the barrier between 0.3 and 0.5. Three things are doubtful. The loosening of cohesion did almost nothing, because the compass decides when stagflation begins from its memory of mergers, and the first bout came in the same year and at the same strength as in a run with the loosening switched off. After the first bout unemployment fell to the floor of the model and the barrier of 0.96 still held the drawing near zero, so the rule let popular resistance outweigh the want of hands. The module was not tried together with the other optional modules. Two runs at 20,000 claims over 40 years with the modules listed in opposite orders gave identical figures.

---

## Optional module: Organised labour

The module is in `modules/labour.py` and is switched on with `--with labour`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. It takes over two jobs that the single number for popular resistance does in the nine modules, and it leaves that number its other jobs. Two optional reads were added. The sabotage module now reads the strength of labour in each industry in place of popular resistance when it sets the level of unemployment at which wages keep pace with prices. The stagflation module now reads the strength of labour in the whole economy in place of popular resistance when it sets the part of each price rise that wages obtain. Both modules read popular resistance exactly as before when this module is off. `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** Chapter 16, "Workers and capitalists", "Patterns" and "The politics of inflation". Chapter 13, the passage on the workers of the bourgs. Chapter 1, the passage on the growth of large units. Bichler and Nitzan, "Can Capitalists Afford Recovery?" (2014), the sections on Figures 9, 10, 15 and 16. Bichler and Nitzan, *The Global Political Economy of Israel* (2002), chapter 3 and chapter 4, "The Histadrut Contra Workers". Nitzan and Bichler, "Going Global" (2001), section 7.3. Bichler and Nitzan, "Growing Through Sabotage" (2020), footnote 35. Jordan Brennan, "Rising Corporate Concentration, Declining Trade Union Power, and the Growing Income Gap: American Prosperity in Historical Perspective" (Levy Economics Institute, 2016), which was downloaded for this module and is in `data/bn_archives/papers/brennan_union_power_2016/`. Brennan was a student of Nitzan, and a search of the authors' online archive for his name and the words "trade union" returned entries. The search also covered the book and every paper on disk.

**What the literature says.** Chapter 16 reports that inflation in the United States moved income from workers to firms for half a century, and then says that this direction is not fixed: "in Germany and France, two countries where labour is relatively strong, the impact of inflation on labour/capital redistribution has been far less clear". The consequences of inflation "depend on the relative power of the leading firms, capital in general and labour groups, among others; this relative power can change over time and so can the distributional outcomes". The same chapter says that under inflation the population "starts to resist and fight back" and "wants to be compensated by higher wages". The 2014 paper reports for the United States from 1930 that "whenever unemployment rises, the rate of change of the real wage falls – and vice versa when unemployment falls". The real wage is the wage measured against prices. The paper speaks of "the role of rising unemployment in disciplining workers", and concludes that "the threat and exercise of unemployment remains central and crucial – not only temporarily as Marx suggested, but permanently". The paper adds a caution: after the early 1940s the level of the real wage rose together with unemployment, so unemployment governs how fast wages move and not where they stand. Chapter 13 describes the first strikes and what met them: "when they revolted (the first known strikes took place in Flanders during the thirteenth century) the retaliation – often coordinated within and between the bourgs – was usually swift and violent", and the class struggle "was never merely an 'industrial dispute'". Chapter 1 names "the growth of large units – big business, large government and organized labour" as one development, and the 2020 paper lists "large labour unions" among the "large power coalitions". The 2002 book warns that a large union is not the same thing as strong labour. Of the Israeli union federation it says: "attempts to boost the central power of the Histadrut were aimed not to empower workers, but to weaken them", and the federation "accepted its role as a restraining factor in the labour market". The same book says that newly drawn-in labour "broke the Histadrut's monopoly". The 2001 paper says how strikes reach owners: "strikes, uprisings, isolation, sanctions, or war" matter "through their expected impact on future profit", and are reduced to "'present value' and 'risk premia'". Brennan's study supplies the only measurements. It separates "the amassment of power from the exercise of power": the part of employees who belong to unions is the first, and strike activity is the second. It reports that "the extent of strike activity is tightly and positively correlated with the rate of growth of hourly earnings", and that "trade union power, manifested in union density and strike activity, progressively redistributes income". It gives two figures for the United States: "in 1900, unionization was below 3 percent", and union membership reached "a historic high of 29 percent in 1954". The literature gives no rule for how organisation grows, how often organised employees strike, or how much a strike wins. It gives no rates.

**Rule.** The rule has ten parts.

1. For each industry the module keeps the part of the employees who are organised. At the start it is 3 per cent in every industry.
2. The real wage is the wage divided by the average price. Each year the module compares the real wage with the best real wage of the last ten years. The grievance is the shortfall divided by 5 per cent, and it is never more than 1. The grievance is the same in every industry, because the model has one wage and one average price.
3. Each year the unorganised part of an industry shrinks by 5 per cent, times the grievance, times a number for the size of the units in the industry. That number is 0.3 in an industry where dominant capital holds nothing and 1 in an industry that dominant capital holds entirely.
4. Each year the organised part of an industry shrinks by its own size times the sum of three things. The first is a fading of 3 per cent. The second is the repression that the organised force module publishes. The third is 5 times the part of the working population that the proletarianization module reports as newly drawn in. The second and third are 0 when those modules are off.
5. Discipline is unemployment divided by 25 per cent, and it is never more than 1. Militancy is the grievance times one minus discipline. The strike activity of an industry is its organised part times militancy. It stands for the part of the industry's employees who are on strike in the year.
6. An organisation that is not striking counts for half its size. In an industry that dominant capital holds entirely it counts for a quarter, and in between the part falls in proportion.
7. The strength of labour in an industry is its organised part, times the sum of the quiet part of step 6 and the remainder multiplied by militancy. When militancy is 1 the strength equals the organised part. The strength of labour in the whole economy is the average over industries, weighted by employees.
8. The sabotage module uses the strength in each industry where it used popular resistance: full strength raises the level of unemployment at which wages keep pace with prices by 5 points. The stagflation module uses the strength in the whole economy where it used popular resistance: full strength gives wages the whole of each price rise.
9. The risk of every claim in an industry is multiplied by 1 plus the strike activity of the industry. The module removes last year's rise each year before it applies this year's, so the rise lasts only as long as the strikes.
10. The module publishes the strength in each industry and in the whole economy, the organised part of all employees, the organised part in the industries that dominant capital mostly holds, strike activity, the grievance, militancy and the real wage.

Popular resistance keeps its other jobs. It still lowers the cohesion of dominant capital, weakens its hold on its target markup, and raises its risk.

**Additions.** The literature supplies six things: the outcome of inflation depends on the strength of labour, the population demands higher wages when prices rise, unemployment disciplines workers, strikes meet force, a large union can be turned against its members, and strikes reach owners as risk. The form of every part of the rule is an addition. Every number is an addition except the starting organisation of 3 per cent, which is Brennan's figure for the United States in 1900. The numbers are the ten years of memory, the shortfall of 5 per cent, the yearly organising of 5 per cent, the figure of 0.3 for small units, the fading of 3 per cent, the weight of 5 on newly drawn-in labour, the level of 25 per cent for discipline, the half that a quiet organisation counts for, the half of that lost in a held industry, and the weight of 1 on risk. Three choices need to be stated. Tying organising to the industries that dominant capital holds rests on a few words about large units and on nothing more exact. Letting unemployment act on strikes and not on organisation follows the caution of the 2014 paper and Brennan's report that union membership surged during the Depression. Brennan's study is not by Nitzan and Bichler, and it borrows the term "countervailing power" from Galbraith, which they do not use. Five things are missing. The model has one wage, so organised employees do not earn more than unorganised employees. A strike costs owners no earnings and raises their risk only. Strikes do not feed popular resistance, and the retaliation of chapter 13 arrives only when the organised force module is on. Brennan reports that organisation rose and fell with particular laws, and the rule has no laws. Co-option is a fixed discount and not a process that dominant capital carries out.

**Expected.** While mergers proceed and prices are steady, the real wage should slip only slightly, the grievance should stay low, and organisation should stay near its starting level. When dominant capital turns to raising prices, the real wage should fall, the grievance should become complete, and employees should organise, fastest in the industries that dominant capital holds. Strikes should be muted while unemployment is high and should spread as hiring recovers. The strength of labour should then give wages a larger part of each price rise and should lower markups, so the real wage should recover. When the real wage has recovered to the best level in memory, strikes should stop and organisation should fade slowly. The risk of dominant capital should rise more than the average risk during a strike wave, because its industries are the most organised, and differential capitalization should fall for that time. A quiet organisation in held industries should count for little, as the book on Israel says of the Histadrut.

**What it showed.** These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 and 119 years, compared with the same runs without the module. Until the first bout of stagflation the figures were nearly the same as without the module: the organised part of employees stood between 2.5 and 5 per cent, and the strength of labour stood between 0.01 and 0.03. In the first bout the grievance became complete, the organised part rose by the year labelled 1935 to 21 per cent of all employees and to 40 per cent in the industries that dominant capital mostly held, and strike activity reached 21 per cent of employees in that year, after unemployment had fallen from 10.6 to 0.5 per cent. The strength of labour peaked at 0.21, which is about half the level of popular resistance that it replaced, so wages recovered more slowly: the capitalist share was 31.3 per cent in 1930 against 28.7 per cent, and 19.1 per cent in 1940 against 12.6 per cent. Strikes were credited with a loss of differential capitalization of 0.12 over fifteen years and with a gain of 0.13 when they stopped, and differential capitalization was 792 in 1940 against 1,069 and 1,609 in 1950 against 1,564. In the run of 119 years a low and steady strike activity of 3 to 6 per cent of employees ran from 1960 to 1990 while markups crept up, organisation stood near 20 per cent, and the last bout began in the same years as without the module and ended with unemployment at 5.5 per cent in 2005 against 14.1 per cent. Four things are doubtful. Strikes stopped altogether between 1935 and 1940, because the real wage had then recovered to the best level of the previous ten years although it was still far below its level before the bout; the ten years of memory is a setting, and this result depends on it. Unemployment stayed at the floor of the model from 1935 to 1950, ten years longer than without the module, because markups stayed high for longer and small claims went on hiring. The strength of labour never came near the level at which wages keep pace with prices, so the runs do not show the case of Germany and France. The module was not tried together with the other optional modules, so the reads of repression and of newly drawn-in labour were not exercised. Two runs at 20,000 claims over 40 years with the modules listed in opposite orders agreed to thirteen significant figures, and the nine modules alone differ between the two orders by a rounding error of similar size.

---

## Optional module: Household debt

The module is in `modules/household_debt.py` and is switched on with `--with household_debt`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. Three optional reads were added. The sabotage module and the stagflation module now multiply their measure of the markup by a number for each industry that this module publishes, so that they do not treat interest on household debt as a markup. The resistance module now lowers popular resistance by a number that this module publishes, times the level of resistance. A run without this module gives exactly the figures it gave before, and this was confirmed against a copy of the three files without the added reads. `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** Chapter 9, "The capitalization of every thing" and "Human beings", with the footnote on micro-credit. Chapter 13, the passage on the separate rate of return of each instrument of credit. Chapter 16, "Workers and capitalists". Bichler and Nitzan, "New Imperialism or New Capitalism?" (2006), the section on capitalization. Tim Di Muzio and Richard H. Robbins, *Debt as Power* (2016), chapters 1, 2, 4 and 5, which was downloaded from the authors' archive for this module and is in `data/bn_archives/papers/di_muzio_robbins_debt_as_power_2016/`. Sandy Brian Hager, *Public Debt, Inequality, and Power* (2016), chapters 1 and 5. The search covered the book and every paper on disk. A search of the online archive and of the wider web for work in this tradition on household debt found the book by Di Muzio and Robbins and nothing else that gives a rule.

**What the literature says.** Chapter 9 says that "every expected income stream is a fair candidate for capitalization", and it uses the household loan as its first example. Workers "borrow money to buy a car, take a mortgage to purchase a home, or open a line of credit to acquire what they otherwise can't with their immediate income". "As borrowers, they commit themselves to repaying the principal and interest, a liability that the lending institution happily discounts as an asset." "To settle their debt the workers need to work (or beg) for an income. In other words, they need to devote part of their life to the bank. And it is this part of the worker's life that the bank capitalizes as an asset on its balance sheet." A footnote adds that "these options are available only to a minority of the world's workers, although 'micro-credit' banks and similar innovations gradually extend the Bernoullian principle even to the most wretched". The 2006 paper says what the worth of such a claim depends on: "the value of her mortgaged home, car, and line of credit together being dependent on her expected lifetime earnings and the risk of her being laid off". The authors say nothing more about household debt. Di Muzio and Robbins use the theory and add to it. They say that lenders gain by finding more borrowers: "one way to expand earnings is by expanding the pool of debtors", and "one of the major inventions here was the creation and development of the consumer and various types of consumer credit 'products'". They say when households borrow: "as wages have stagnated or fallen for the majority in mature economies, there has been an increasing reliance on consumer credit to finance necessities like food, medicine, and electricity". They say that lending costs the lender nothing, because "borrowers are not borrowing money that exists when they use bank products, but money created on computer screens". They say that lending is an exclusive right held by few: "the entire commercial banking system depends on this type of exclusive right to issue credit", and banks "have what could be called an 'effective monopoly'". They say that "there is always more debt in the system than the ability to repay", and that lenders "can send in lawyers, judges, and police". They report that household debt in the rich countries rose to 130 per cent of the income that households keep after tax by 2007. Hager says what the borrowing does to resistance. "Access to cheap credit allows low- and middle-income Americans to maintain consumption habits in the face of decades-long wage stagnation." "Household debt serves as a compensatory mechanism", and widespread credit "helps to dampen resentment toward the top percentile". He reports that the debts of the lower half of United States households rose from 0.61 of their income in 1989 to 1.37 in 2007. The literature gives no rule for how fast households borrow, what they pay, or who among the lenders receives it. It gives no rates.

**Rule.** The rule has ten parts.

1. The module keeps the part of wage earners to whom lenders extend credit. It is 10 per cent at the start. Each year the part not yet reached shrinks by 0.2 times the top hundred's part of all employees.
2. The module works out the wage that households keep. It takes the wage, removes the part that was pledged as interest in the previous year, and divides by the average price. It compares the result with the best level of the last ten years. The shortfall is the part by which the present level stands below that best level.
3. Households borrow to make up half of the shortfall. The new borrowing of the year is one half, times the reach of part 1, times the shortfall, times the wage bill.
4. One tenth of the outstanding principal is repaid each year.
5. Household loans carry twice the normal rate of return. The interest of the year is that rate times the principal.
6. Interest cannot take more than 15 per cent of the wage bill. Principal above that limit is written off. A fall in employment shrinks the wage bill and so brings the limit nearer.
7. Each industry receives interest in proportion to its earnings before interest, multiplied by a weight. The weight is 1 in an industry where the hundred largest claims hold nothing. It is 5 in an industry that they hold entirely, and it rises in proportion in between. Every claim in an industry receives the same proportion of its other earnings.
8. The module scales the earnings per employee of every claim in an industry so that the earnings of the industry include exactly this year's interest. The module remembers the proportion it put into each industry, and the next year it removes that proportion and puts in the new one. Lending takes nothing from the earnings of the lenders.
9. The module publishes a number equal to twice the year's new borrowing as a part of the wage bill. The resistance module lowers popular resistance by that number times its level.
10. The module publishes the capitalized value of the debt, the principal, the pledged part of the wage bill, the reach, the shortfall, the new borrowing, the write-offs, the top hundred's part of the interest, and the capitalist share of income with the interest counted once. The capitalized value is the interest divided by the normal rate of return and by a risk of 1 plus the rate of unemployment. The module also publishes, for each industry, the part of earnings that comes from the markup alone.

The wage in the ledger is not changed, because the ledger has no claim that stands for wage earners. The core's own readout of the capitalist share adds the interest to earnings and does not take it from wages, so it counts the interest twice in total income. The readout of part 10 counts it once.

**Additions.** The literature supplies five things: a household loan is a capitalized claim on a part of the borrower's working life, lenders enlarge their earnings by enlarging the number of borrowers, households borrow when wages fall behind, the borrowing dampens resentment, and the worth of the claim depends on the risk of lay-off. The form of every part of the rule is an addition, and every number is an addition. The numbers are the starting reach of 10 per cent, the rate of 0.2 at which reach spreads, the ten years of memory, the half of the shortfall that is borrowed, the tenth that is repaid, the multiple of 2 on the normal rate of return, the limit of 15 per cent, the weight of 5, the multiple of 2 for placation, and the weight of 1 on unemployment in the risk of the claim. Three choices need to be stated. The model has no banks, so part 7 places lending in the industries that dominant capital holds, and every claim in such an industry gains, including the small ones.
