The sabotage module already treats the markup in the same way. Measuring the shortfall against the best level of ten years means that households stop borrowing ten years after a fall in the wage, even if the wage never recovers. Di Muzio and Robbins are not Nitzan and Bichler, and their account of money created as debt is used only to say that lending costs the lender nothing. Six things are missing. Households do not spend what they borrow, so the module says nothing about what can be sold. Di Muzio and Robbins hold that interest is passed into prices and forces growth, and the rule has neither. The claim is not traded. The discipline that debt imposes on an indebted worker does not reach the module for organised labour. Mortgages, student loans and card debt are not told apart. A claim bought from another industry carries the interest proportion of the industry it leaves, so its other earnings are slightly misread after a merger across industries.

**Expected.** While mergers proceed and prices are steady, wages should slip only slightly against prices, and the debt should stay very small. Reach should spread as the top hundred come to hold more employees. When dominant capital turns to raising prices, the wage that households keep should fall, households should borrow, and the pledged part of the wage bill should rise for as long as the bout lasts. The capitalist share of income should then be higher than without the module, and most of the interest should go to the industries that dominant capital holds, so differential capitalization should be higher too. The borrowing should hold popular resistance down, so wages should recover more slowly and the bout should last longer or end with a higher capitalist share. After the bout the debt should be repaid over about ten years, and the gain of dominant capital from interest should be given back. The limit of part 6 should be reached only in a long bout with heavy unemployment.

**What it showed.** These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 and 119 years, compared with the same runs without the module. Until the year labelled 1920 interest took less than 0.2 per cent of the wage bill and the two runs were nearly the same. In the first bout of stagflation the wage that households kept fell 22 per cent below its best level, new borrowing reached 7 per cent of the wage bill in a year, and interest rose to 2.6 per cent of the wage bill by 1935, with principal equal to a quarter of a year's wages and 57 to 64 per cent of the interest going to the top hundred. Popular resistance stood at 0.35 in 1930 against 0.47 and at 0.21 in 1935 against 0.43, the capitalist share was 30.3 per cent in 1930 against 28.7 and 16.8 per cent in 1940 against 12.6, and differential capitalization was 480 in 1930 against 436 and 808 in 1935 against 751. A trial with placation switched off gave a capitalist share of 29.8 per cent in 1930 and 13.5 per cent in 1940, so the interest itself added about one point and the slower recovery of wages came from the dampening of resistance. The module was credited with a gain in differential capitalization of 0.09 over the fifteen years of the bout and a loss of 0.05 over the fifteen years of repayment. In the run of 119 years the last bout began in the same year and unfolded differently: by 2005 interest took 4.8 per cent of the wage bill, the principal was 0.44 of a year's wages, popular resistance was 0.48 against 0.94, and the capitalist share was 40 per cent against 30 per cent with the bout still at full strength. Four things are doubtful. The debt never came near the levels that Di Muzio, Robbins and Hager report, which are above one year's income, because borrowing stops ten years after a fall and half the shortfall is a setting. The limit of part 6 was never reached, so no principal was written off in any run. After 1940 the two runs differ in the timing of small later bouts, and part of that difference comes from which claims are among the hundred largest and not from any stated effect of the debt. The module was not tried together with the other optional modules. Two runs at 20,000 claims over 40 years with the modules listed in opposite orders agreed to fifteen significant figures.

**Changed on 26 November 2026.** The module now owns an income component named "household interest" and sets it each year to the interest that a claim receives for each employee (see "How modules share a quantity"). The interest is no longer part of the markup, so the sabotage and stagflation modules no longer need the published part to leave it out. The income is registered as paid out of the wage bill. The core's own readout of the capitalist share therefore counts the interest once, and the statement in this section that the core counts it twice is no longer true. A newly founded claim starts with no interest and receives its part in the following year. The figures under "What it showed" come from before the change. In a run at 60,000 starting claims over 60 years, the two runs gave the same figures to 1905, before households had borrowed much. In the year labelled 1950 differential capitalization was 1,738 after the change and 1,712 before it, and the capitalist share was 11.5 per cent against 11.4.

**Changed again on 26 November 2026: the payer is recorded.** The module no longer sets its income component. It proposes one transfer each year, in which wage earners pay the interest and the claims receive it in the proportions of part 7, and the core settles it (see "The record of transfers" in the section "How modules share a quantity"). Wage earners cannot pay more than the wage bill, counting every transfer they pay. The module's own limit of 15 per cent of the wage bill is unchanged. The amount for each employee is now reckoned on the employees a claim has after the year's hiring. At 300,000 claims over 60 years, differential capitalization was 1,362, 3,140 and 7,974 in the years labelled 1920, 1935 and 1950, where it had been 1,362, 3,138 and 8,008. The highest capitalist share was 30.2 per cent in both.

## Optional module: Hierarchy inside the firm

The module is in `modules/hierarchy.py` and is switched on with `--with hierarchy`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. It is a readout. It proposes no change to any claim, no existing module reads what it publishes, and no existing module was edited. `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** Blair Fix, "A Hierarchy Model of Income Distribution" (Working Papers on Capital as Power, 2018), sections 2 to 5 and appendices A, B, D and E. Blair Fix, "Hierarchy and the Power-Law Income Distribution Tail" (2018), which prints the same equations. Both were downloaded from the authors' archive for this module, records 540 and 546, and are in `data/bn_archives/papers/fix_hierarchy_model_2018/` and `data/bn_archives/papers/fix_hierarchy_power_law_tail_2018/`. *Capital as Power* (2009), chapter 14, the passage that follows equation 4. The search also covered the book and every paper on disk for the word "hierarchy". Nitzan and Bichler use the word often and give no rule for pay inside a corporation.

**What the literature says.** Chapter 14 explains why the book measures a corporation by its employees: "Ever since they first emerged in the power civilizations of the ancient river deltas, hierarchical organizations have been measured by 'head', or capita." "The number of 'heads' under one's immediate command – relative to the number of heads commanded by others – is indicative of one's immediate power." The book says nothing about how the heads inside one corporation are arranged or paid. Fix supplies that. He builds on the book by name: "Building on Nitzan and Bichler's capital as power hypothesis, I propose that capitalist income is derived from power – hierarchical power. By owning firms, capitalists earn the legal right to helm firm hierarchies. From this position of power, capitalists can partition firm income streams as they see fit." He measures the power of a person inside a firm by counting subordinates: "I propose that one's power within a social hierarchy is proportional to the number of subordinates under one's control." His model of a firm rests on three assumptions taken from studies of the payrolls of a few real firms. "Firms are hierarchically structured, with a span of control that increases exponentially with hierarchical level." The span of control is the number of people in one rank divided by the number in the rank above. "The ratio of mean pay between adjacent hierarchical levels increases exponentially with hierarchical level." The third assumption is that pay inside one rank is spread in the same way in every rank. Fix writes these as equations. The span of control between rank h-1 and rank h is a times the exponential of b times h. The average pay of rank h is the average pay of rank h-1 times r to the power h. The top rank is the last rank that holds at least one person. Fix says what kind of model this is: "The hierarchy model is not built on micro principles. It is not dynamic, and it is not agent based." "It is essentially an extrapolation." He reports what it finds: "the tail of the distribution is almost completely determined by hierarchy", and "without large, hierarchically organized firms, there would be no power law distribution of top incomes". A power law here means that the number of people with a given income falls off as a fixed power of the income, and that power is called the exponent. Fix also reports that the pay of chief executives in the United States rose after 1980 together with the part of income going to the best paid one per cent and with the dividend share of income, and he reproduces this by raising the number r. Both papers print the equations. Neither prints the fitted values of a, b or r as figures. The values are drawn in charts, and the code and data are in a separate deposit that could not be reached.

**Rule.** The rule has seven parts. Parts 1, 3, 5 and 6 are Fix's equations.

1. Rank 1 is the bottom of a claim. The number of people in rank h is the number in rank 1 divided by every span of control up to rank h, rounded down to a whole number. The span of control between rank h-1 and rank h is 1.2 times the exponential of 0.125 times h. The top rank is the last rank that holds at least one person. The same two numbers apply to every claim.
2. The ledger gives the total employees of a claim and not the size of its bottom rank. The module works out the total for a long list of bottom ranks and reads the bottom rank back from the total, as Fix does. The bottom rank then takes whatever is left after the higher ranks are counted, so the ranks add up to the claim's employees exactly. A claim with fewer than one employee has one rank.
3. The average pay of rank h is the average pay of rank h-1 times 1.1 to the power h.
4. The module reads the one wage of the model as the average pay of every claim. It sets the pay of the bottom rank of each claim so that the pay of all its ranks adds up to its wage bill. The pay of the top rank is therefore taken out of the same wage bill.
5. The pay of the top rank of a claim, divided by the wage, is the ratio that Fix calls the CEO pay ratio.
6. The hierarchical power of the top rank is the employees of the claim divided by the number of people in the top rank.
7. The module publishes twelve readouts every year. Three concern ranks: the number of ranks in the claim of the average employee, in the hundred largest claims, and in the claim with the most ranks. Four give the pay of the top rank as a multiple of the wage: in the median claim, in the claim at the 99th percentile, in the hundred largest claims, and in the claim where it is highest. One gives the hierarchical power of the top rank in the hundred largest claims. Four describe pay among all employees: the part of all pay that goes to the best paid one per cent, the part of those people who work in the hundred largest claims, the Gini index of pay, which is 0 when everyone is paid the same, and the exponent of a power law fitted to the pay of the best paid one per cent with Fix's estimator.

**Additions.** The literature supplies the form of the rule and none of its numbers as printed figures. The two numbers for the span of control, 1.2 and 0.125, are the middle of the ranges on the axes of Fix's Figure 29, which runs from 0.8 to 1.6 for the first and from 0.075 to 0.175 for the second. They are readings of a chart and not fitted values, so both settings are additions. Fix gives every firm its own value of r, drawn from a distribution fitted to the pay of chief executives in a commercial database, and his charts draw it between 1.0 and 1.3. The module uses 1.1 for every claim, and that is an addition. Fix gives every firm its own average pay, and the module gives every claim the one wage of the model. Letting the bottom rank take the remainder is an addition that is needed because employees in the ledger are not whole numbers. Using the hundred largest claims for the readouts on dominant capital follows the model, where Fix uses the 350 largest firms. Five things are missing. Pay does not differ inside a rank, so the top of the distribution is a set of steps. The number r is fixed, so the module cannot show the rise of top pay together with the capitalist share that Fix reports, and a rule that tied r to the markup would be a rule Fix does not give. Fix's proposal that the part of a person's income that comes from ownership rises with hierarchical power is not built, so the earnings of a claim are not handed to its top ranks. Government organs and businesses that are not corporations are not in the ledger, so their hierarchies are not counted. Nothing reads the readouts, so popular resistance does not respond to the pay of the top rank.

**Expected.** Every other figure of a run should be exactly the same with the module on or off. At the start, when claims are of similar size, the pay of the top rank should be a modest multiple of the wage everywhere, and the best paid one per cent should receive only a few times what their number would suggest. As mergers build large claims, the largest claims should gain ranks, and the pay of their top rank should rise in jumps, because each added rank multiplies it. The best paid one per cent should come to be found mostly inside the hundred largest claims, and the fitted exponent should fall, which means a fatter tail. A bout of stagflation should leave the readouts almost unchanged, because it changes markups and not the number of employees of the large claims.

**What it showed.** These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 and 119 years. The printed output of `full.py` was identical, character for character, with the module on and off, as a readout requires, so the comparison is between years of one run. The hundred largest claims went from 8 ranks in the year labelled 1890 to 10 by 1920 and to between 11 and 12 by 1990, and the pay of their top rank went from 19 times the wage to 109, to 300 around 1970 and to 680 in 1995. The pay of the top rank depended almost only on the number of ranks: the highest in any claim stood at 109 times the wage for forty years, then at 308, then at 962. The best paid one per cent received 5.5 per cent of all pay in 1890 and 9.1 per cent in 1995, the part of them who worked in the hundred largest claims rose from 1 per cent to 62 per cent, and the fitted exponent fell from 7.4 to 3.0. The first bout of stagflation raised the capitalist share from 11 to 29 per cent and left every readout of pay nearly unchanged, which shows what the fixed value of r leaves out. Three things are doubtful. The levels depend heavily on r: with 1.05 the top rank of the hundred largest claims was paid 29 times the wage in 1995 and the best paid one per cent received 3 per cent of all pay, and with 1.2 the figures were 60,000 times and 56 per cent. The pay of the top rank in the median claim fell from 4.7 to 1.9 times the wage, because the many newly founded claims have only a handful of employees and two or three ranks. The span of control rests on a reading of a chart, and the module was not tried together with the other optional modules, although the only thing it reads that they change is the number of employees.


---

## Optional module: The shaping of wants

The module is in `modules/wants.py` and is switched on with `--with wants`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. It reads only the ledger. No existing module was edited, and `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** Chapter 9, "Human beings", the passage on the consumer and its footnote. Chapter 12, "Strategic sabotage", "The markup and the target rate of return" and "The differential underpinnings of universal sabotage". Chapter 7, "Financial intermediation, advertising and insurance". Chapter 11, "The degree of confidence". Bichler and Nitzan, "Dominant Capital and the New Wars" (2004). Bichler and Nitzan, "Growing Through Sabotage" (2020). David Troy Cochrane, "Differentiating Diamonds: Transforming Knowledge and the Accumulation of De Beers" (Working Papers on Capital as Power, 2017). James McMahon, "Is Hollywood a Risky Business? A Political Economic Analysis of Risk and Creativity" (2018). The last two were downloaded from the authors' archive for this module, records 495 and 554, and are in `data/bn_archives/papers/cochrane_differentiating_diamonds_2017/` and `data/bn_archives/papers/mcmahon_hollywood_risky_business_2018/`. The search covered the book and every paper on disk for advertising, salesmanship, persuasion and the consumer.

**What the literature says.** Chapter 9 says that capitalism shapes what people want and, more than that, "makes these 'preferences' sufficiently predictable for capitalists to translate them into expected profit discountable to present value". It says that "this shaping – i.e. the very creation of a predictable 'representative' consumer" is what "gets capitalized". The same passage gives the only figures. "Capitalists worldwide are estimated to spend up to $600 billion annually just to remind us of these options. If we assume a 15 per cent markup on these advertising expenditures, we get $90 billion of net profit. This sum represents roughly 5 per cent of global net corporate income". Ninety billion dollars is 5 per cent of 1,800 billion dollars. The outlay of 600 billion dollars is therefore one third of net corporate income. The passage adds that the estimate leaves out "sales promoting expenditures buried in the 'cost of production'". Chapter 12 places advertising among the means of exclusion. "For the profits of one owner (or a coalition of owners) to beat the average, others must be prevented from accessing the same earnings." The means "include direct limitations, such as predatory pricing, formal and informal collusion, advertising and exclusive contracts". "For the benefiting owner, the differential gain accrues because the necessary industrial limitation is borne by other owners." Chapter 7 says that advertising, "like every social activity", is "invariably differential". Chapter 11 says that the large capitalists "try to tame volatility" and are "risk shapers". The 2004 paper says that the differential profit of General Motors "depends on a sophisticated propaganda war which creates wants and shapes desires". Cochrane applies the theory to one campaign. "In order to beat the average, capitalists engage in a struggle of differentiation. Advertising is part of that process." The response that a seller hopes for "is a differential purchase. In other words, sellers wish for buyers to choose their product rather than another." He reports that the profit of De Beers fell against the average until its national campaign of 1939 and rose against it for three decades afterwards, and he calls advertising "an important, but uncertain, piece" of that reversal. McMahon reports that the large film studios lowered their risk after 1980 "both absolutely and differentially, relative to dominant capital". He ascribes this to making the behaviour of audiences "more predictable", and he says that the studios depend on "the habitual return of audiences". The literature gives no rule for how much any claim spends, for what the spending does to a markup, or for how long its effect lasts. It gives no rate except the one third.

**Rule.** The rule has eight parts and works inside each industry.

1. Each claim lays out a part of its earnings each year on shaping wants. The customary part is one third. A claim whose markup stands below the markup of its industry raises its part by 10 per cent of itself in that year. One twentieth of any part above the customary one lapses each year. The part is never below one third and never above one half.
2. The weight of a claim's campaign among the buyers of its industry is its outlay raised to the power 1.25. A large campaign therefore counts for more than the same money divided among many small campaigns. The claim's part of the summed weights of its industry is its part of the year's persuasion.
3. Each claim carries a hold on the buyers of its industry. Each year the hold moves one fifth of the way toward the claim's part of the year's persuasion. A newly founded claim starts with no hold. The buyer in a merger keeps its own hold and does not receive the hold of the claim it buys.
4. The lead of a claim is its part of all the holds in its industry, divided by its part of the industry's employees, minus one. A claim with a lead above zero holds more of the buyers than of the capacity. The leads of an industry, weighted by employees, add up to zero. The rule counts no lead above 1.
5. The rule calls for a difference between the markup of each claim and the markup of its industry. The difference, as a fraction of the industry's markup, is 0.2 times the lead, less the amount by which the claim's outlay part exceeds the average outlay part of its industry. A claim that lays out more than its industry does on average pays the excess from its own earnings. The module measures how far the markups of the industry already differ in this pattern, and it adds what is missing. It never removes a difference. What it adds to some claims it takes from the other claims of the same industry, so the earnings of the industry do not change.
6. The average outlay of an industry is a cost that every seller puts into the price. The module owns a cost component, the outlay on wants. Each year it sets that cost, for every claim of an industry, to the industry's average outlay part times the industry's markup. That is the industry's outlay for each employee, counted in wages. The core adds it to the wage and the markup when it works out the price.
7. Each year a claim with a lead above zero loses a part of the amount by which its risk stands above 1. The part is 0.1 times the lead. A claim without a lead gets no relief.
8. The module publishes five readouts: the outlay of all claims as a part of all earnings, the average outlay part of the claims outside the hundred largest, the average lead of the hundred largest claims, the difference in markup that the rule calls for in the hundred largest claims, and the year's fall in the risk of the hundred largest claims from part 7.

The module adds two numbers to every claim, the outlay part and the hold. When every claim of an industry has a hold in proportion to its size, every lead is zero, and all the outlay of the industry yields nothing but its cost in the price.

**Additions.** The literature supplies five things: wants are shaped, the shaping is differential, it makes buyers predictable, predictability is capitalized, and the limitation is borne by other owners. It prints no figure that the rule can use as it stands. The customary outlay of one third is an addition worked out from chapter 9. The book says that capitalists worldwide spend "up to $600 billion" a year on advertising, and that a profit of $90 billion on that sum is "roughly 5 per cent of global net corporate income", which implies an income of about 1,800 billion. The book calls these sums "tentative". The rule uses an upper estimate for the world in one year as the usual level of every claim. The form of every part of the rule is an addition, and every other number is an addition. The other numbers are the ceiling of one half, the step of 10 per cent, the lapse of one twentieth, the power 1.25, the one fifth by which a hold moves, the 0.2 of part 5, the limit of 1 on the lead, and the 0.1 of part 7. Four choices need to be stated. The power above one in part 2 is the only reason why a large claim gains a lead from the same outlay part as a small claim, and no passage states it. The rule that a trailing claim raises its outlay applies the book's phrase "not to maximize but to exceed" to this one tool, and no passage states it either. Part 6 lets the whole average outlay pass into the price. Part 5 only adds a difference, so that it does not undo the lead that the stagflation module gives the hundred largest claims. A difference that a hold no longer supports wears away through the cost cutting module, which draws every claim back toward the level of its industry. Seven things are missing. The outlay is paid to nobody, because the ledger does not tell the claims that sell persuasion apart from other claims, so the 5 per cent of corporate income that the book ascribes to them does not appear. The general effect that chapter 9 describes, a whole population made predictable for all sellers, is left out, because the rule lets the gains cancel when all claims do the same. The module does not make the markup of a claim steadier from year to year, and the relief of part 7 stands in for that. A hold does not bar entry: a new claim is founded as before and then earns less than its neighbours for a few years. A hold cannot be bought together with the claim that built it. Fear, which chapter 9 names as the second path beside the promise of pleasure, is not in the rule. Popular resistance does not respond to the shaping of wants.

**Expected.** At the start all claims are of similar size, every lead should be near zero, and the module should do almost nothing. In every industry about half the claims trail in any year, so the outlay should creep above one third everywhere and prices should rise a little, with no gain to anyone. As mergers build large claims, the large claims should gain a lead over their small neighbours, because a large campaign counts for more. Their markup should then stand above the markup of their industry, and the small claims of the same industries should earn less, trail, and lay out more. The earnings per employee of the top hundred relative to the average should be higher than without the module, and their risk should be a little lower. The capitalist share of income and unemployment should hardly change, because the module only moves earnings between the claims of one industry. A claim that grows fast by buying should have a smaller lead than its size suggests, because it buys employees and not holds. Where dominant capital comes to hold most of an industry, its lead should shrink toward zero, because it is then the average of that industry.

**What it showed.** These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 years, compared with the same run without the module. During the merger wave the markup of the top hundred stood 5 to 6 per cent above the markup of their own industries. In the year labelled 1920 their earnings per employee were 1.66 times the average against 1.59, their risk relative to the average was 0.88 against 0.91, and differential capitalization was 472 against 445. The module was credited with a gain in differential capitalization of 0.10 to 0.13 in every five years of the wave, and the cost cutting module took back 0.06 to 0.08 of it, where it had taken back almost nothing without the module. The outlay rose from one third of earnings to 0.41 within ten years, and to 0.43 among the claims outside the top hundred, while the top hundred stayed near one third. A trial without the rule that trailing claims raise their outlay gave the top hundred about half the difference in markup, so half of their gain came from their lead and half from the extra outlay that the small claims paid. The lead of the top hundred was at most 0.21 in the years printed and fell to 0.02 by 1950, because they bought employees faster than they built holds and because they came to hold more of their industries. A trial with the power in part 2 set to 1 gave the top hundred a lead below zero and a markup 5 to 7 per cent below their neighbours during the wave, and differential capitalization of 423 in 1920. The whole gain of the large claims therefore rests on that one added number. The capitalist share of income and unemployment were the same in both runs until 1940. After that a small later bout of stagflation was somewhat stronger with the module, and differential capitalization ended at 1,489 against 1,564, which is a difference in timing and not a stated effect of the rule. Three things are doubtful. The relief of risk was small: a trial without part 7 gave 464 in 1920 against 472. The module was not tried together with the other optional modules, and the module for price leaders also sets differences in markup by size inside an industry. Two runs at 20,000 claims over 40 years with the modules listed in opposite orders printed the same figures and agreed to fourteen significant figures.

**Changed on 26 November 2026.** The module now owns a markup component named "wants premium", and each year it sets that component to the difference that the claim's lead and outlay call for (see "How modules share a quantity"). Before the change the module added what was missing from that pattern, took nothing away, and left it to the cost cutting module to wear a difference down. The premium now follows the hold from year to year, and the cost cutting module does not wear it down. The statements in this section about those two points, and the figure for what cost cutting "took back", come from before the change. The premium is a member of the overlap group "hold on buyers". In a run at 60,000 starting claims over 60 years, differential capitalization in the year labelled 1920 was 487 after the change and 472 before it, and in 1950 it was 1,531 against 1,489. The capitalist share in 1950 was 12.0 per cent against 12.7. Later on the same date the module stopped multiplying prices (see "How the price is worked out"). It now owns a cost component named "outlay on wants", as part 6 of the rule above describes. The premium of a claim now moves that claim's price, because the core works every price out from the markup. Before, the premium moved no price. The outlay is now in the price as a level, from the first year, and it moves with the industry's markup as well as with the outlay part. In a run of 60 years from 300,000 claims every figure except the price level is the same as before. The price level starts at 1.133 where it started at 1.100, and in the last year it is 1.86 where it was 1.77. The outlay averaged between 0.033 and 0.161 of the wage.


---

## Optional module: Eruptions that change the rules

The module is in `modules/eruptions.py` and is switched on with `--with eruptions`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. Four optional reads were added, one each in the sabotage, stagflation, merger and resistance modules. Each read multiplies a number of that module by one minus a number that this module publishes. All four numbers are 0 when this module is off, and a run without this module gives exactly the figures it gave before. `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** Bichler and Nitzan, "Growing Through Sabotage" (2020), sections 8.1 and 11.1 with footnote 33. *Capital as Power* (2009), chapter 1, "The capitalist creorder and humane society"; chapter 11, "The degree of confidence"; chapter 17, the last page before the postscript and the postscript. Bichler and Nitzan, "Systemic Fear, Modern Finance and the Future of Capitalism" (2010), the sections on systemic fear and on the future of capitalism. The search covered the book and every paper on disk. Nothing was downloaded.

**What the literature says.** The 2020 paper separates two kinds of resistance. Resistance "takes two forms: power-replacing and power-negating. The two forms can coexist in actual struggles, but they are clearly distinct conceptually." The first kind "is internal to the power struggle itself": governments, corporations, labour unions and other organisations "constantly contest each other's power". The second kind "is external to the power struggle proper. It originates not from a quest for power, but from its very negation – namely, the desire to undo hierarchy altogether in favour of autonomy and cooperation. This latter drive is often latent, buried under the dead weight of hierarchical power, but it is always there". The same paper says how the second kind shows itself: "Most of the time, this quest for autonomy is latent and largely invisible. But occasionally it erupts, usually unpredictably, to stir society, if only for a brief historical moment, toward greater creativity, cooperation and self-rule." A footnote lists such eruptions, from the English Civil War and the French Revolution to May 1968 and the Arab Spring. The paper adds that after such a shift "the ever-present Machiavellian force is likely to kick back", so that hierarchy returns. The book ties the patterns of accumulation to the confidence of the rulers. The patterns "show the conviction of the rulers that they are in command – and that those whom they command cannot resist. This conviction, though, is never certain. Confronted with sufficient opposition, whether explicit or implicit, it can crumble. And when it does crumble, the result is to shatter the 'determinism' of the rulers, the patterns of their differential accumulation and, possibly, the entire state of capital that defines their rule." The postscript says where that confidence shows: "power is confidence in obedience", and "in modern capitalism, this certainty is manifested in the ability of capitalists to control and systematize the upward movement of prices". The 2010 paper describes the loss of confidence. "When the ruling class is no longer certain of its ability to govern, it becomes indecisive; indecision inhibits ruthlessness; lack of ruthlessness fuels opposition; and effective opposition is the other side of disintegrating rule." It says how the moment is prepared: "redistribution requires greater sabotage and larger doses of force, and as social stress builds up, the stage is set for the crucial inversion". Of the 1930s it says that "the rulers were no longer confident in the obedience of the ruled", and that "the very institution of capitalization was put into question, so even if future earnings could somehow be predicted, it didn't seem certain that future ownership claims on these earnings could be priced and transacted". Chapter 11 says that the risk coefficient expresses, inversely, "the degree of confidence capitalists have in their own predictions". The book also sets a limit on any rule of this kind. The humane society "exists mostly as an unknown potential", and "occasionally, though, it erupts, often without warning, to challenge and sometimes threaten the institutions of capitalist power. These eruptions – and their consequences – do not follow a pre-set pattern. They cannot be systematically theorized." The language of capitalist power "can never anticipate when and how they will erupt". The literature gives no rule and no rate, and it says that there can be none.

**Rule.** The module holds one number, the level of the eruption, between 0 and 1. The level is 0 in ordinary years. The rule has six parts.

1. Each year in which popular resistance stands at or above 0.4 adds one to a count of years. A year below 0.4 puts the count back to zero.
2. In each year without an eruption, the chance that an eruption begins is 10 per cent times the count of years. The module decides by a random draw from its own stream. Sustained resistance therefore makes an eruption likely, and the year in which it comes is not fixed in advance.
3. An eruption stands at the level 1 for three years. After that the level loses half of itself each year, and it is put to zero when it falls below 0.05. No new eruption can begin while one lasts. The count of years starts again from zero afterwards.
4. While the level is above zero, four rules of other modules are suspended in proportion to the level. The sabotage module loses the grip of dominant capital on its target markup, so the industries that dominant capital holds drift toward the normal markup. The stagflation module loses its intensity, so there are no concerted price rises and no cut in hiring. The merger module loses its buying, so no claim is bought. The resistance module loses the repression that the organised force module supplies, so force wears down no resistance.
5. The risk of every claim is multiplied by 1 plus one half of the level. The module removes last year's rise before it applies this year's, so the rise lasts only as long as the eruption. The module also publishes the level under the name `systemic_fear`, which the optional hype and risk shaping modules already read.
6. The module publishes the level, the count of years, and the number of eruptions so far.

The module invents no new rule, and it cannot. An eruption in the literature is the appearance of something new, which the authors call autonomy, cooperation and self-rule. The module can only switch off, for a time, rules that the model already has. What people build while the rules are off is outside the model. When the eruption fades, the old rules return exactly as they were.

**Additions.** The literature supplies five things: resistance that negates power is distinct from resistance inside the rules, it erupts occasionally and briefly, sufficient opposition can make the confidence of the rulers crumble, the patterns of accumulation are then shattered, and hierarchy tends to return. The form of every part of the rule is an addition, and every number is an addition. The numbers are the threshold of 0.4, the 10 per cent for each year, the three years, the half lost each year, the level of 0.05, the four settings that say how much of each rule a full eruption suspends, which are all 1, and the one half for risk. Four choices need to be stated. The book says that eruptions "cannot be systematically theorized", and a rule that makes an eruption likely after some years of high resistance goes against the letter of that passage. The rule keeps the timing open by a random draw, and it rests on the other passages, which speak of "sufficient opposition" and of stress that "builds up". The threshold of 0.4 was chosen because popular resistance in the nine modules peaks between 0.4 and 0.5 in an ordinary bout of stagflation, so a higher threshold would give no eruption in a run of 60 years. The module measures the latent drive toward autonomy by the same number that measures resistance inside the rules, although the 2020 paper calls the two "clearly distinct conceptually", because the model holds only one level of popular resistance. The book says that the population is "impartial to breadth", and the rule still stops the buying of claims, on the ground of the sentence of the 2010 paper about ownership claims that could not be "priced and transacted". Six things are missing. The module closes no boundary and breaks up no claim. An eruption cannot end the rule of capital, although the book says that it possibly can. The old rules return unchanged, where the 2020 paper expects the returning hierarchy to stand higher than before. An eruption cannot be put down by force before it has run its course. The fear of an eruption does not act on the rulers before one begins. The 2016 paper on the stock market measures systemic fear by the way owners price shares, and this module only sets that number equal to the level of the eruption.

**Expected.** While popular resistance is low, the level should be zero and every figure should be the same as without the module. An eruption should come some years into a bout of stagflation, because a bout is what raises popular resistance. The bout should then stop at once, prices should stop rising, hiring should recover, and wages should gain on prices. Mergers should stop for the same years, so the top hundred should hold a smaller part of all employees afterwards. The markups of the industries that dominant capital holds should fall toward the normal level, so differential capitalization should fall, which is what the book means by the shattering of the patterns of differential accumulation. All capitalization should fall by about a third while the eruption is at full strength, and the differential measure should not be moved by that fall, because the fear is the same for every claim. Popular resistance should fade after the eruption, because the inflation and the unemployment that fed it are gone. When the rules return, the compass should find that mergers have been low, and it might turn dominant capital toward price rises again, which would be the return of hierarchy that the 2020 paper describes.

**What it showed.** These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 and 119 years, compared with the same runs without the module. Through the year labelled 1932 the two runs were identical. Popular resistance passed 0.4 in 1929, and the first eruption began in 1933, when resistance inside the rules had already ended the bout and the intensity of stagflation was down to 0.03, so the eruption had no bout left to stop. Its effects were the smaller ones: no claim was bought for three years, capitalization measured against the wage fell by a third and recovered, the top hundred held 16.2 per cent of employees in 1935 against 17.3 per cent, and differential capitalization was 694 in 1935 against 751 and 1,400 in 1950 against 1,564. The small later bout that the run without the module had near 1945 did not occur, because the years without buying lowered the volume of mergers that the compass treats as usual. In the run of 119 years a second eruption began in 2004, after popular resistance had stood above the threshold for six years and had reached 0.93, while a bout was still under way. Prices stopped rising at once, unemployment fell from 18.7 to 8.3 per cent in four years, the capitalist share was 18.5 per cent in 2009 against 21.5 per cent, and the wage measured against prices was 0.82 against 0.79. Four things are doubtful or count against the run. Differential capitalization ended higher with the second eruption, at 8,551 in 2009 against 6,822, because every bout of stagflation in this model lowers the differential measure and the eruption cut the bout short; the eruption therefore did not shatter differential accumulation, and by this measure it rescued dominant capital. Popular resistance stayed above 0.85 through the second eruption, because unemployment was still high, and the intensity of stagflation was rising again by 2009, so the old rules were returning into the same conditions. In an ordinary bout the resistance that works inside the rules ends the bout at about the level where the threshold stands, so whether an eruption changes much depends on that one setting and on the draw; a trial with the threshold at 0.3 began the first eruption only one year earlier. With the organised force module also on, popular resistance peaked at 0.27 in 60 years and no eruption occurred, so the stay of repression was never exercised, and the module was not tried with the other optional modules. Two runs at 20,000 claims over 60 years, each with one eruption and with the modules listed in opposite orders, printed identical figures.


---

## Optional module: Energy capture and hierarchy

The module is in `modules/energy.py` and is switched on with `--with energy`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. It keeps an account of energy in joules beside the money ledger, and it proposes no change to any claim. One optional read was added to the resistance module, which adds a number called `energy_grievance` to the year's rise in popular resistance. That number is 0 when this module is off, and a run without this module gives exactly the figures it gave before. `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** Bichler and Nitzan, "Growing Through Sabotage: Energizing Hierarchical Power" (2020), sections 1.1, 3.1, 8, 9, 10, 11 and 12, with footnote 32 and Table 2. Blair Fix, "Energy and Institution Size" (PLoS ONE, 2017), sections 2 and 4 and appendices H and I. Blair Fix, "Putting Power Back Into Growth Theory" (Review of Capital as Power, 2015), the section "Hierarchy and Growth". The two papers by Fix were downloaded from the authors' archive for this module, records 500 and 444, and are in `data/bn_archives/papers/fix_energy_institution_size_2017/` and `data/bn_archives/papers/fix_power_growth_theory_2015/`. A third paper by Fix, "Energy, Hierarchy and the Origin of Inequality" (2019, record 597), was downloaded into `data/bn_archives/papers/fix_energy_hierarchy_inequality_2019/` and was only skimmed; the rule takes nothing from it. The search also covered the book and every paper on disk for the word "energy". The book of 2009 has no account of energy.

**What the literature says.** The 2020 paper starts from a count of energy and not of money. It divides the energy that a society converts in a year by the number of people, and it calls the result energy capture per head. It then divides that energy by its use. "Our starting point in modelling the relationship between energy conversion and hierarchy formation is to split the total energy captured/converted by society into two distinct flows: (1) hierarchical energy that serves to impose, sustain and augment hierarchical power, and (2) livelihood energy that provides for subsistence and wellbeing." The paper measures the flows in gigajoules per head per year. A gigajoule is one billion joules. It writes the part of all energy that goes to hierarchy as H. The paper says why hierarchy uses energy. "Since the exertion of hierarchical power against opposition necessitates strategic sabotage, and because sabotage requires energy to be carried out, hierarchical structures, regardless of their specific purpose, consume energy. Everything else being the same, the more hierarchical the institution or organization, the greater the energy needed simply to erect and sustain it." The activities it lists are "standing armies, organized religion, education and conditioning, a legal system, various bureaucracies, police forces, ongoing propaganda". It adds that "greater resistance tends to require more energy to sustain any given level of hierarchy", and that this energy is "over and above that consumed by the higher strata of the hierarchies". The paper says that the two flows are judged by different yardsticks. The population judges livelihood energy by its amount, "more joules". The rulers judge hierarchical energy by its share, "a greater decimal share of total energy". The paper then names four paths that a society can follow. A society is expanding when the share H and livelihood energy per head both rise. It is in crisis when H rises and livelihood energy falls. It is in decline when both fall. It is democratizing when H falls and livelihood energy rises. On the passage into crisis the paper says: "as long as livelihood energy per capita is rising (Trajectory A), most subjects remain busy with their daily lives, paying little or no attention to expanding power, rising hierarchy and growing sabotage", but "when livelihood energy actually drops (Trajectory B1), the clash between wellbeing and hierarchical power comes to the fore". The paper gives no way to measure either flow. It says that the split "is bound to be coloured by our political, ideological and theoretical disposition", that different observers will place the same society at different points, and that only the paths can be agreed on. It calls its own framework "too rudimentary to allow further analysis". The paper also explains why it does not make one unit the measure of everything. Of utility and of labour time it says that "the very idea that a conflictual society can be understood with a single universal measure is an invitation for trouble". Fix supplies the measurements. He reports four regularities across countries and across a century of the United States. "As energy use per capita increases: 1. The small firm employment share declines; 2. The large firm employment share increases; 3. The mean firm size increases; 4. The government employment share increases." He prints one formula, in a caption of his appendix: "The formula is Epc = 14.3 L^1.02, where Epc is energy per capita and L is mean firm size." Energy is in gigajoules per head per year. He reports that the sectors of United States industry lie on the same line, with energy measured for each worker, and that they "extend (by two orders of magnitude) the trends found at the national level". The 2020 paper sums up his finding in one sentence: "larger organizations are able to capture (and also tend to consume) more energy per capita than smaller ones". Fix also gives a simple picture of a firm. "All firms are 'ideal' hierarchies with a single span of control." The span of control is the number of people in one rank divided by the number in the rank above. "All individuals in and above the third hierarchical level are considered 'managers'." The managers' part of a firm then rises with its size and approaches one divided by the square of the span. Fix and the authors disagree about the reason for the link between energy and hierarchy. Fix holds that large hierarchies are needed to capture more energy. The authors hold that hierarchy is sought for its own sake and is limited by the energy that can be had: "the size and height of hierarchical institutions are capped by their ability to capture energy".

**Rule.** The rule has nine parts. It reads the employees of each claim, the working population and the level of popular resistance.

1. The energy that one employee converts in a year is 14.3 gigajoules times the number of employees of the employee's claim raised to the power 1.02. The energy of a claim is that figure times its employees. The energy of the society is the sum over all claims. A person without work converts no energy in this account. A claim with more than 3,000 employees converts for each employee what a claim of 3,000 converts.
2. Every claim is a chain of command with a span of control of 2.5. The people in the third rank from the bottom and above are managers. The managers' part of a claim's employees follows from Fix's equations. It is zero in a claim that is too small to have three ranks, and it approaches 16 per cent in a very large claim.
3. The managers' part of a claim's energy is hierarchical energy.
4. The hierarchical energy of part 3 is multiplied by one plus the level of popular resistance. At full resistance a hierarchy therefore needs twice the energy.
5. If the organised force module is on, the energy converted in military jobs is hierarchical energy too. The part that parts 3 and 4 already counted is not counted twice.
6. Hierarchical energy is the sum of parts 3 to 5. Livelihood energy is all energy less hierarchical energy. The share H is hierarchical energy divided by all energy.
7. The module compares the share H, livelihood energy per head and all energy per head with their values five years earlier. It names the path with the four names of the paper. Crisis and democratization each have two forms, one with more energy per head in all and one with less.
8. When livelihood energy per head falls from one year to the next, the module publishes one half of the fall, as a fraction, under the name `energy_grievance`. The resistance module adds that number to the year's rise in popular resistance. A fall of 10 per cent in one year therefore adds 0.05 to popular resistance.
9. The module publishes ten readouts every year: all energy in joules, all energy per member of the working population in gigajoules, hierarchical energy per head, livelihood energy per head, the share H, the part of all energy that is converted inside the hundred largest claims, the part of all hierarchical energy that is used inside them, the name of the path, the same as a number, and the grievance of part 8.

Energy enters no price, no markup, no earnings and no capitalization. Part 8 is the only place where anything in the model reads the account, and it acts on popular resistance. With the setting of part 8 at zero the module is a readout and every other figure of a run is unchanged.

**Additions.** The literature supplies the two flows, the share H, the four paths, the statement that command and the containing of resistance use energy, one printed formula, and Fix's picture of a firm. Everything that turns these into numbers for single claims is an addition. Fix estimated his formula on the average size of firms in whole countries, and the rule applies it to each claim. The two numbers of the formula, 14.3 and 1.02, are therefore used for a different quantity from the one Fix fitted them to, and their use as settings for single claims is an addition. Two closer applications were tried first. The formula applied to the average claim of the whole ledger made energy per head fall by more than half in sixty years, because the model founds many small claims and the average claim shrinks. The formula applied to the average claim of each industry made energy per head rise twentyfold without a limit on size, and it made energy fall whenever small claims were founded. The limit of 3,000 employees is a reading of Fix's statement about two orders of magnitude and is not a printed figure. Counting a person without work as converting nothing is an addition. Counting the managers' part of a claim's energy as hierarchical energy is an addition, because Fix uses the managers' part of employment as a sign of hierarchy and does not turn it into energy. The span of 2.5 is the middle of the 2 to 3 that Fix uses in his appendix, and he prints no fitted value. The doubling at full resistance, the five years of part 7 and the one half of part 8 are additions, and so is the form of parts 4, 5 and 8. The rule also takes one observer's view of what counts as hierarchical energy, and the paper says that no view is the objective one. Eight things are missing. The limit that the authors stress is not built: nothing holds a merger back for want of energy, because the literature gives no rule for how much energy can be had. The opposite effect, in which too much hierarchy lowers the energy captured, appears only through unemployment. The ledger holds no government organs, so the government employment that Fix measures is absent, and so are the churches, schools, courts and police that the paper lists. The self-employed and the businesses that are not corporations are not in the ledger, so the claims are far larger than the firms in Fix's data and the level of energy per head is far too high. Propaganda is not counted, although the optional module on the shaping of wants holds a money outlay for it, because turning money into joules would need a price of energy. The hierarchies that the paper places outside formal organisations, in markets and networks, are not counted. The energy lost in conversion is not kept apart. The rulers do not respond to the share H, although the paper says that the share is their yardstick.

**Expected.** During a merger wave, energy per head should rise, because the same employees sit in larger claims. The share H should hardly move during the wave, because claims of a hundred employees already have nearly the full managers' part. The hundred largest claims should come to convert most of the energy. The path should be named expanding. In a bout of stagflation, unemployment should lower the energy captured, popular resistance should raise the energy that hierarchy needs, and the share H should rise while livelihood energy per head falls. The path should then be named a crisis, and the grievance of part 8 should add to popular resistance. When resistance fades after the bout, the share H should fall and livelihood energy should recover, which the paper's table names democratizing, although nothing in the model has become more democratic. With the organised force module on, military hiring should keep energy per head rising through a bout, so that the path stays expanding while the share H rises further.

**What it showed.** These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 and 119 years, compared with the same runs without the module. With the setting of part 8 at zero, differential capitalization in every printed year was the same as without the module. In that run energy per head rose from 4,700 gigajoules in the year labelled 1895 to 12,300 in 1920 and 15,900 in 1950, and the hundred largest claims converted 22 per cent of all energy in 1895 and 70 per cent in 1920. The share H stood between 0.158 and 0.164 through the whole merger wave, rose to 0.23 in the first bout of stagflation, and fell back to 0.20 by 1950. The path was named expanding until 1925, a full crisis around 1930, and democratizing from 1940. With part 8 at its setting of one half, the run was the same as the run without the module until the first bout began. The grievance then added about 0.008 a year to popular resistance, which stood at 0.52 in 1930 against 0.47, and the later course differed in timing: a small second bout near 1945 was stronger, and differential capitalization was 1,404 in 1950 against 1,564. In the run of 119 years, livelihood energy per head fell from 23,800 gigajoules in 1995 to 15,600 in 2005 during the last bout, and the share H rose from 0.19 to 0.32. With the organised force module on, the share H reached 0.27 in the first bout and the path stayed expanding, because military hiring kept energy per head rising. Four things are doubtful. The level of energy is twenty to fifty times the record of the United States, and it depends almost wholly on the added limit: a limit of 300 employees gave 3,300 gigajoules per head in 1950 and a limit of 30,000 gave 130,000. The share H follows popular resistance and little else, because Fix's managers' part is nearly the same for every claim above a few dozen employees; a span of 2 gave a share of 0.31 in 1950 and a span of 3 gave 0.14. The name democratizing is given to years in which resistance only faded. The grievance repeats in part what unemployment already adds to popular resistance. Two runs at 20,000 claims over 40 years with the modules listed in opposite orders agreed to fifteen significant figures. The module was tried together with the organised force module only.

---

## Optional module: Externalisation

The module is in `modules/externalisation.py` and is switched on with `--with externalisation`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. It keeps an account of burdens that no claim's books carry. One optional read was added to the resistance module, which adds a number called `externalisation_grievance` to the year's rise in popular resistance. That number is 0 when this module is off, and a run without this module gives exactly the figures it gave before. `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** DT Cochrane, "From Colonialism to Climate Change: The Power to Externalize" (2017), record 519 of the authors' archive. DT Cochrane, "Disobedient Things: The Deepwater Horizon Oil Spill and Accounting for Disaster" (*Valuation Studies*, 2020), record 636. Jeffrey Monaghan and DT Cochrane, "Fight to Win! Tools for Confronting Capital" (2012), record 667. All three were downloaded for this module and are in `data/bn_archives/papers/cochrane_power_to_externalize_2017/`, `cochrane_disobedient_things_2020/` and `monaghan_cochrane_fight_to_win_2012/`. *Capital as Power* (2009), chapter 9 on climate change and discounting, chapter 12 on unemployment, chapter 13 under "Absorptiveness", and chapter 16 under "Cost cutting" and "Input prices". The archive lists 24 items by Cochrane. Judged by their titles, the others concern De Beers, General Electric, profits during the pandemic, oil supply and theory, and they were not read for this module.

**What the literature says.** The archive holds the 2017 talk as a video and a one-page abstract. It holds no paper of the talk. The abstract is the whole of the written source, and it reads: "The history of capitalism is a history of externalization. While benefits were tallied, costs were routinely excluded from accounts. In this presentation I examine the history of externalization as a power process. A history of brutality and destruction marketed as innovation and efficiency, externalization has been the shadowy counterpart to accumulation. From colonialism to climate change, I will describe how negative effects of economic activity are systematically imposed on vulnerable communities via the interconnected processes of production and accounting. Finally, I will consider how externalities have made their way back onto the books through uprisings, court rulings and environmental disasters." The abstract makes four statements. Costs are kept out of the accounts. Keeping them out is an exercise of power. The costs fall on those who are least able to refuse them. The costs can come back by three routes. Cochrane's paper on the oil spill of 2010 shows one return in detail. He writes that the costs of the disaster "would reduce expected earnings and could increase the risk to earnings". He reports that BP's capitalization, measured against the share index, fell until it was "just over half its pre-disaster value", and that the company then recovered "about 20 percent of its relative pre-disaster value" within twenty days. The chapter by Monaghan and Cochrane describes a return by campaign. Nike "sought to boost earnings by pushing down costs", and "it was hardly unique in this", but "its high profile led anti-sweatshop activists to focus their attention" on it. The company had gained on dominant capital by 13.5 per cent a year, and it then lost 28 per cent a year for three years. The book of 2009 has no account of externalisation. It says three things that bear on it. Chapter 13 says that owners watch "whether environmental policies raise the effective penalty for polluters", and that "any regular effect on profit is instantly discounted into present value". Chapter 9 says that capitalists "contribute to climate change, while on the other they cheerfully discount the boom in the doom", and it shows that damage a hundred years away is worth almost nothing today at an ordinary discount rate. Chapter 16 says that cutting costs "helps dominant capital meet the average, not beat it", because "the benefits quickly tend to spill over to other firms". The literature gives no rule for how much is shifted, onto whom, or how fast it returns. The fall of BP is its only figure.

**Rule.** A burden is a consequence of a claim's activity that somebody has to bear. A burden is on the books when the claim pays for it. It is off the books when somebody else bears it without payment. The module measures a burden as a yearly amount for each employee of the claim, in units of the yearly wage. The rule has nine parts.

1. There are three bearers. The first is the claim's own employees. The second is the communities around the claim, which means everybody else alive now. The third is later years, which means the people who will bear a consequence after it has built up. Every burden in the account is tagged with the claim that shifted it and with one of these bearers.
2. Each bearer has a weakness between 0 and 1. The weakness of communities is one minus the level of popular resistance. The weakness of employees is the same number times unemployment divided by 10 per cent, and never more than the weakness of communities. The weakness of later years is always 1.
3. The power of a claim is its capitalization divided by the average capitalization of the hundred largest claims, and never more than 1. Every claim has 30 per cent of the full power to shift whatever its size. The other 70 per cent is in proportion to the power of the claim.
4. Each year each claim shifts onto each bearer 0.15 per cent of the wage for each employee, times the weakness of the bearer, times the claim's power to shift, times the room left under a ceiling. The ceiling is 1.5 per cent of the wage for each bearer. Shifting a burden raises the claim's earnings per employee by the amount shifted times the wage. The price of the claim does not change.
5. Each year a part of the burdens on employees and on communities returns to the books. The part is 20 per cent times the level of popular resistance for a claim without power, and twice that for a claim with full power. A returned burden lowers earnings per employee by the same amount times the wage. It also raises the risk of the claim by the fraction of its earnings that the return took away.
6. The burden on later years builds up into a stock for each claim. Each year each claim has a chance of a disaster equal to 5 per cent times its stock, measured in years of the wage for each employee, and never more than 5 per cent. A disaster puts the claim's whole burden on later years back on its books, multiplies its risk by 1.8, and empties its stock. The emptied stock falls on the communities in that year.
7. The rise in risk that the module has given a claim fades by 20 per cent a year.
8. The module adds up the burdens that employees and communities bear in the year, and the stock that disasters released, for each member of the working population. It publishes 0.3 times that sum. The resistance module adds the number to the year's rise in popular resistance. Burdens on later years add nothing until a disaster releases them.
9. The module publishes eleven readouts. They are the burdens off the books as a part of all earnings, the burden on each of the three bearers, the stock, the burden per employee of the hundred largest claims divided by the average, the amounts shifted and returned in the year, the number of claims struck by a disaster, the number of those among the hundred largest, and the grievance of part 8.

No date is entered and nothing comes from outside. The chance of a disaster is produced by what the claim itself has shifted, and the returns of part 5 are produced by the resistance that the burdens themselves feed.

**Additions.** The literature supplies the account, the direction of each effect and the three routes of return. Every rule that turns these into numbers is an addition. The three bearers are an addition, and so is the rule for the weakness of each. The measure of a claim's power to shift is an addition. The book says that cost cutting does not let dominant capital beat the average, and Cochrane calls externalisation a power process, so the rule lets size count for 70 per cent of the power to shift, and that goes beyond the book. The yearly attempt, the ceiling, the rate of return, the doubling for the most powerful claims, the rise in risk for each unit of earnings lost, the chance of a disaster, the fading of risk and the rate of the grievance are additions. The factor of 1.8 on risk is taken from one case: it lowers the capitalization of a struck claim to about 0.55 of its level, which is Cochrane's "just over half" for BP. One case is not a general figure, so the setting is an addition. The rule that a released stock falls on communities is an addition. In a merger the buyer keeps its own burden for each employee, as if it carried its own practice into what it bought, and a new claim starts with nothing off the books. Six things are missing. Colonialism is in the title of the talk and is not in the rule, because the model has one country and so has no bearer abroad. The content of the talk beyond its abstract was not available as text and was not used. The rule does not say what a burden is made of. The bearers lose nothing that the ledger counts: their wage and their employment are unchanged, and only their resistance responds. A disaster strikes one claim, although Cochrane shows that the drilling moratorium lowered the value of other oil companies as well. The sabotage module reads all earnings as markup, so it treats the earnings from a shifted burden as a higher markup and applies its own rules to them.

**Expected.** At the start no bearer resists, so every claim should shift burdens, and the larger claims should shift more for each employee. Shifting should be credited with a small gain in differential capitalization, and the gain should shrink as the ceiling is approached and as cost cutting spreads the gain through each industry. The burdens on employees and communities should raise popular resistance a little in quiet years, where the nine modules alone leave it near zero. Higher resistance should send burdens back to the books, and it should send more back from the largest claims, so the credit to externalisation should turn negative during a bout of stagflation. The burden on later years should not return through resistance at all. It should build up and return only through disasters, which should become more frequent as the stock grows. A disaster in one of the hundred largest claims should cost that claim about half of its capitalization for a few years.

**What it showed.** These results come from runs at 60,000 starting claims over 60 years, compared with the same run without the module. The burdens off the books grew to 19 per cent of all earnings by the year labelled 1920, fell to 5 per cent by 1930 when popular resistance stood at 0.53, and stood at 10 per cent in 1950. The hundred largest claims carried 2.1 times the average burden per employee in 1895 and 1.2 times in 1935. The module was credited with a gain in differential capitalization of 0.07 in the first five years and 0.03 in the next five, and with between minus 0.02 and plus 0.02 in every later period, so externalisation let dominant capital beat the average only at the beginning. Popular resistance stood at 0.07 in 1920 against 0.03 without the module and at 0.53 in 1930 against 0.47, and the later course differed in timing: a second bout near 1945 was stronger, with unemployment at 8.0 per cent against 5.1 per cent, and differential capitalization was 855 in 1945 against 1,093 and 1,809 in 1950 against 1,564. The burden on later years reached about 0.95 per cent of the wage and stayed there, its stock reached 0.28 years of the wage for each employee by 1950, and disasters rose from 18 claims in 1895 to about 1,900 claims a year in 1950, with between none and four of the hundred largest claims struck in any year. Five things are doubtful. Unemployment was lower in the first years, 4.2 per cent in 1900 against 4.8 per cent, because the sabotage module read the extra earnings as higher markups and small claims hired more. The size of every effect follows from the added ceiling, which lets burdens reach nearly a fifth of all earnings. The burden on later years never falls for the economy as a whole, because a struck claim is free to begin shifting again the next year. With the grievance set to zero, popular resistance before the first bout was the same as without the module, so the standing resistance of quiet years is the grievance alone. The module was not tried together with any other optional module. Two runs at 20,000 claims over 40 years with the modules listed in opposite orders gave identical figures.

**Changed on 26 November 2026.** The module now owns an income component named "burdens off the books" and sets it each year to the burdens that the claim has shifted, times the wage (see "How modules share a quantity"). The gain is no longer part of the markup. The sabotage module therefore no longer reads it as a higher markup, and small claims no longer hire in answer to it. The cost cutting module no longer spreads the gain through the industry, so the sentence in the module's own text that says it does is no longer true. The income is registered as an income of its own, because nobody in the ledger pays it, and the core adds it to total income. The figures under "What it showed" come from before the change. In a run at 60,000 starting claims over 60 years, the capitalist share in the year labelled 1920 was 12.6 per cent after the change and 10.8 before it, because the gain now stays with the claims that shifted the burden and is not competed away. Differential capitalization in 1920 was 430 against 431. In 1950 it was 1,675 against 1,809, and the capitalist share was 11.6 per cent against 10.6.

---

## Optional module: Ownership of nature

The module is in `modules/nature.py` and is switched on with `--with nature`. It runs beside the nine modules and replaces none of them. Two existing modules received one optional read each. The sabotage module and the green-field module both read a number called `nature_open`, which is the part of each industry that the owners of a natural resource leave open to newcomers. That number is 1 when this module is off, and a run without this module gives exactly the figures it gave before. `core.py` was not changed.

**Source.** *Capital as Power* (2009), chapter 16, the footnote on "peak commodities" and the section "The stagflation norm". Jonathan Nitzan and Shimshon Bichler, "Bringing Capital Accumulation Back In: The Weapondollar-Petrodollar Coalition" (1995), sections 4 and 5, in `data/bn_archives/papers/weapondollar_petrodollar_1995/`. The book's footnote cites pages 487 to 492 of this paper. Jonathan Nitzan and Shimshon Bichler, *The Global Political Economy of Israel* (2002), chapter 5, in `data/bn_archives/papers/gpe_israel_2002/`. Shimshon Bichler and Jonathan Nitzan, "Still About Oil?" (*Real-World Economics Review*, 2015), record 432 of the authors' archive, downloaded for this module into `data/bn_archives/papers/still_about_oil_2015/`.

**What the literature says.** The book's footnote states the principle. "Mother Nature does impose real limits, but natural limits do not determine prices. Ownership does. Recall that price implies restriction, and restriction presupposes ownership. This prerequisite means that natural limits can have no bearing on prices unless they are mediated through the power conferred by ownership." Things without owners, such as "the ozone layer, clean water and ocean fish stocks", are "as physically limited as oil is", and yet "they cannot be made 'scarce'". "By contrast, oil is tightly owned, mostly by governments of oil-producing countries and large petroleum companies. And it is the historical variations in the relative power of these owners - exercised mostly through the high politics of crisis and war - that explains why the price of oil varies so dramatically while its actual 'availability' does not". The footnote ends: "even when oil finally becomes nearly exhausted, there will be nothing to prevent its owners from giving it away for free if they so wished." The 1995 paper gives the evidence. The ratio of proven reserves to production "rose by a quarter" from the 1960s to the early 1990s, while "the real price of crude oil was three to four times higher than what it was in the 1960s", "an increase which must hence be ascribed to institutional factors". Its chart of inventories "does not betray any systematic relationship between 'scarcity' and price movement". The paper describes two periods. In the first, which it calls the era of "free flow", a few large companies "all but completely dominated" the trade. Then "the growing number of independent producers exerted downward pressure on prices", and "with a growing number of actors, counteracting this threat solely via corporate collusion became increasingly difficult". In the second period, which it calls the era of "limited flow", the number of players grew "from less than a dozen in the 1960s, to over 150 by the late 1970s", and yet "collective action was in fact more effective with hundreds of participants during the 1970s and 1980s, than with only a handful before the onset of the crisis". The paper's reason is that the price depends on "perceived scarcity", and that conflict in the Middle East supplied it. Militarization "helped maintain prices even in the absence of tight producer coordination". "Despite the absence of any real shortage, the onset of these wars generated a crisis atmosphere which was sufficient to create a fear of shortage and cause prices to rise. Similarly, the diffusion of a 'crisis' - either at the end of a war, or when it became clear that its continuation posed no danger to the flow of oil - the price began to stagnate or fall." The 2002 book describes the loss of cohesion. In the 1980s "the OPEC front was no longer united", "rivalry prevailed instead of cooperation", and "the likelihood of restoring OPEC's earlier cohesion seemed remote". The war between Iran and Iraq "forced both to stretch production to the limit", and "from a certain point onward" it "turned from a blessing to a curse". High prices also "induced greater energy efficiency, substitution to alternative sources, and further exploration and output by non-OPEC producers". Saudi Arabia cut its output from 9.8 million barrels a day in 1981 to 3.2 million in 1985 in order to hold the price. The 2015 paper says that "the price of oil has little to do with the commodity's actual scarcity, however estimated", and that in the oil business "profits and incomes are 'all in the price'". It reports a correlation of 0.83 between the relative price of oil and the earnings of the oil companies measured against the earnings of all listed firms. The literature gives directions and one usable figure, the "three to four times". It gives no rule for cohesion and no rates.

**Rule.** The module holds no reserves, no output and no physical limit. It holds the cohesion of the owners of each resource industry, which is a number between 0 and 1. The rule has eight parts.

1. A setting names a few industries as resource industries. Every claim in a named industry is an owner of the resource, and so is every claim that is later founded there.
2. Each year the module measures the tightness of ownership in each resource industry. The tightness is the part of the industry's employees that its seven largest claims hold. It rises when claims of the industry are bought up, and it falls when new claims enter.
3. The module reads the violence of the year. The violence is the intensity of stagflation, or the level of conflict of the coalitions module, or the level of organised force, whichever is highest. Dominant capital produces all three. When the violence rises from one year to the next, an atmosphere of crisis rises by the same amount. Every year the atmosphere loses a fixed part of itself, whether the violence has ended or continues.
4. The cohesion of the owners is the tightness of ownership, plus the atmosphere of crisis applied to the part that tight ownership leaves open. A few owners can act together without a crisis. Many owners can act together only while an atmosphere of crisis lasts.
5. With no cohesion a resource industry sells at the normal markup. With full cohesion it sells at a markup that makes its price a fixed multiple of the price at the normal markup.
